11 Durable Execution

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why must an agent write down what it is about to do before it does it?

Real agent tasks, such as migrating an enterprise database or debugging a distributed codebase, run for minutes, hours, or days, long enough that process restarts, node preemptions, and rolling updates stop being rare events and become part of the workload. The harness that supervises such a task keeps its control record in the memory of a single worker, and the model’s context holds only a curated transcript, so one crash erases the admitted operations, held reservations, and pending approvals that tell a successor what may happen next. Restarting from the original prompt is worse than slow, because it resamples a stochastic model and may repeat an external effect, such as a deployment or a payment, that has already escaped. Durable execution answers with an ordering discipline inherited from transaction processing. The runtime appends every decision and observation to a log that outlives the worker, makes each intended action durable before dispatching it, and rebuilds control state by folding that history rather than by asking the model again. The log still cannot report what happened to a request whose reply never arrived, and keeping that ambiguity visible instead of guessing it away is what lets state survive a long horizon without corrupting the world the agent acts on.

Learning Objectives
  • Construct a trajectory log whose fold rebuilds control state without treating recorded claims as world truth
  • Apply intent-before-dispatch ordering under a declared failure domain, price its acknowledgment, and classify what each crash position lets recovery conclude
  • Select a checkpoint representation and cadence from write volume, capture pause, and reconstruction cost
  • Design a resume policy that reconciles outstanding obligations under current ownership within a stage-by-stage latency budget
  • Distinguish time-travel replay of a recorded projection, harness regression replay, and counterfactual branches by the conclusion each supports
  • Diagnose the first divergence between two executions from captured inputs, stating what the evidence cannot establish
  • Design retention policies that keep the bytes each declared recovery, replay, and diagnostic promise requires
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

11.1 Worker Failure Mid-Trajectory

Horizon stretches state past one worker’s life, so continuation needs a durable record.

Consider a software repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) that has admitted and dispatched an integration test against ws_r18, a fixed workspace snapshot of candidate patch r18. Before the test runner reports a result, the worker process crashes, or the host suffers a spot preemption (the sudden reclamation of an ephemeral cloud node). A replacement worker cannot simply re-invoke the model with the original prompt. Starting over discards context: the pending test invocation, the active capability tokens and resource reservations, any pause state acknowledged by an operator, and any evidence indicating whether the remote executor accepted, completed, or failed the job. Worse, the original test suite may still be executing on a remote cluster, incurring financial charges and modifying sandbox state, while a newly launched model redundantly dispatches a conflicting command. This crash, with its dispatched but unreported test, is the chapter’s running worksheet, and each section asks what the surviving record says about it.

The agent harness already tracks most of what the replacement worker requires. Its live trajectory record separates pending execution continuations from outstanding external obligations, while its admission checks govern which actions may cross the runtime boundary. In a naive implementation, however, both structures reside exclusively in volatile host memory and vanish the instant the worker fails. The model’s context window cannot substitute for this operational state. A large language model (LLM) context contains only an episodic transcript shaped by bounded observation windows and prompt compaction; it contains no record of in-flight network sockets, admitted capability tokens, or active cloud billing meters. As the execution horizon expands across dozens of turns and long-running external tasks, worker crashes, node preemptions, and rolling updates shift from rare anomalies to operational certainties.

↩︎ The trajectory record that holds pending continuations and outstanding obligations is established in The Trajectory Record.

Surviving mid-trajectory failures requires durable history: an append-only, ordered log of every decision, admitted action, and received observation. With this durable log, a replacement worker can deterministically reconstruct the agent’s state, reconcile side effects that already reached external systems, and safely resume execution under valid authority. That capability names the chapter’s subject. A trajectory has durable execution when a different worker, after any failure the runtime declares it tolerates, can reconstruct the trajectory’s control state and outstanding obligations from surviving records and continue it without losing an effect that already escaped or repeating one unsafely. One ordering rule makes that possible. Under write-ahead durability, the record of an intended action becomes durable before the action is dispatched over the network. The rule is the runtime’s mechanical answer to the horizon and state exposures, a bound placed below the model that no prompt can supply. It carries measurable costs in acknowledgment latency on every dispatch, capture pauses at checkpoints, and reconstruction time on recovery.

The chapter builds the durable runtime in the order a crash demands it. First, the trajectory log (section 11.2) defines the record from which a clean worker rebuilds control state, and durable intent before dispatch (section 11.3) orders that record against external effects while pricing the acknowledgment each failure domain requires. Folding an unbounded log and reconstructing mutated files from scratch would grow too slow over long runs, so checkpoints (section 11.4) bind the control record to workspace snapshots at regular strides. The resume protocol (section 11.5) then turns that reconstructed state into an authorized continuation under a new owner. The same durable records then serve offline debugging. Replay (section 11.6) inspects or tests history without new model calls, divergence localization (section 11.7) finds the earliest boundary where two executions disagree, and retention (section 11.8) decides which of these recovery and replay promises the stored bytes can still keep. Each mechanism answers the central question of the worksheet crash by establishing what the surviving record proves and what ambiguity remains unresolved.

11.2 The Trajectory Log

A replacement worker can rebuild the crashed trajectory only from what the failed worker wrote somewhere that outlived it, so the first design question is what outliving it means. If the worker host and its attached disk are destroyed together mid-turn, a replacement on an independent machine can resume only if the acknowledged history was already replicated to independent storage. Durability is therefore meaningful only relative to an explicit fault model that names which failures the stored history must survive. Systems engineers call this boundary a failure domain, the set of faults across which a persistence guarantee holds. Three tiers of increasing severity recur: a process crash that leaves host storage intact, replacement of the worker node while a shared network-attached volume survives, and permanent loss of that storage tier. Each tier demands stronger replication and wider physical fault isolation, and section 11.3.1 prices the acknowledgment each one requires. The crash worksheet targets the strongest tier, in which the acknowledged event history and every artifact required for recovery survive the simultaneous destruction of the worker host and its attached disk. Every durability claim in this chapter is made relative to that contract.

11.2.1 The log as recorded evidence

Surviving the failure domain solves where the bytes live, but it leaves open what those bytes must record so the replacement worker never has to ask the model what happened before the crash. Re-prompting the model from its last visible message cannot reconstruct past control state, because stochastic sampling and a changed external environment steer the trajectory down a different path. Instead, the runtime decouples execution state from process lifetime by recording an append-only, chronological history of every decision and observation across the trajectory.

Definition 11.1: Trajectory log

Trajectory log is the append-only, ordered sequence of typed events (including delivered model prompts, returned completions, resource authorizations, control transitions, and observed tool outcomes) from which a deterministic reducer reconstructs an agent trajectory’s control state.

  1. Significance: Recovery folds recorded decisions sequentially rather than re-executing completed turns. A crash late in a long trajectory therefore costs only deterministic replay time. It avoids fresh model invocations, which would re-expose the system to the compounding error rates of equation 1.1 and accumulate redundant token charges against the task attempt budget in equation 1.2.
  2. Distinction: The trajectory record (definition 10.2) is the live in-memory projection that governs the immediate next decision. The trajectory log is the immutable, append-only history from which that record is derived. The context window is the transient, curated token sequence supplied to the model.
  3. Common pitfall: Recording only tool results while omitting operation dispatches. If an admitted tool call crashes mid-execution or drops its network response, the absence of an intent record leaves the recovery worker unaware that an external side effect may already be in flight.

Formally, the log is a sequence

\[\LoggedEventPrefix = [\LoggedEvent_1, \LoggedEvent_2, \dots, \LoggedEvent_j]\]

When an agent refines an earlier hypothesis or encounters an unexpected tool error, it appends fresh evidence to the tail of the log rather than mutating historical records in place.1 The active trajectory record represents an accumulated projection over this append-only stream. Starting from a base state \(\ProjectedTrajectoryRecord_0\), a deterministic reducer applies each recorded event sequentially:

1 Append-Only Log as Primary Record: Rosenblum and Ousterhout’s log-structured file system treated the sequential log as the primary on-disk data structure, boosting write throughput and simplifying crash recovery (Rosenblum and Ousterhout 1992). A trajectory log inherits these exact advantages: append operations never overwrite historical evidence required by future recovery passes, so a recovering worker can reconstruct state by streaming an ordered log rather than untangling conflicting in-place updates.

Rosenblum, Mendel, and John K. Ousterhout. 1992. “The Design and Implementation of a Log-Structured File System.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 10 (1): 26–52. https://doi.org/10.1145/146941.146943.

\[\ProjectedTrajectoryRecord_j = \text{fold}(\ProjectedTrajectoryRecord_0, \LoggedEventPrefix) = \text{apply}(\dots\text{apply}(\text{apply}(\ProjectedTrajectoryRecord_0, \LoggedEvent_1), \LoggedEvent_2)\dots, \LoggedEvent_j)\]

In this formulation, \(\LogPositionIndex\) indexes the durable log sequence boundary rather than the interaction turn \(\TurnIndex\). Given deterministic reducer semantics and complete event payloads, folding reconstructs the precise control state without invoking the foundation model or re-executing external tools. This architecture embodies the classic event sourcing pattern (Kleppmann 2016): historical decisions serve as deterministic inputs to state reconstruction, while unrecorded events remain explicitly unknown. If the latest durable log entry indicates that a test execution was authorized and dispatched, but contains no corresponding completion event, the reconstructed state marks that operation as unresolved (figure 1). For the worksheet crash, the fold keeps the held reservation and the dispatched test_r18 intent, and it invents no outcome for the test.

Engineers must distinguish the trajectory log from the model’s active context window. Context assembly is selective and ephemeral: prompt compilation filters past interactions, and context compaction periodically replaces verbose command outputs with concise summaries. The trajectory log, by contrast, preserves the exact delivered inputs and operational decisions required by the system’s replay contract. For a truncated observation, the log retains the exact byte slice exposed to the model rather than the full, unpruned output stream emitted by the tool. State reconstruction reproduces only what was durably captured. A compacted summary replays as a summary, and a logged hash of a payload can verify those bytes only while the stored payload itself survives (section 11.8.2 develops this separation of digests from bytes).

11.2.2 The log event schema

If a log entry records only that a tool was called without capturing the worker lease, control epoch, or reservation bound, a replacement worker cannot tell whether a late callback belongs to an invalidated turn or how much budget remains locked. An event log supports reliable recovery only when its schema captures the operational metadata that succeeding workers need to answer those questions. Table 1 groups these event families by their roles during reconstruction. These schemas define logical event boundaries rather than requiring isolated physical storage writes for every individual field. For example, a high-throughput runtime can coalesce authorization checks, budget reservations, and tool dispatch intents into a single batched write transaction, provided the deterministic reducer preserves their causal dependencies. The storage engine must also durably acknowledge the batch before dispatch (the ordering that section 11.3 develops).

Every event envelope contains a trajectory identifier, a unique event identifier, a monotonically increasing sequence_number, schema type and version tags, and tenant or issuer provenance. In addition, the log records the active reducer version alongside the worker lease identity under which appends were accepted. Within this envelope, the sequence_number defines total event ordering, and record_version tracks state machine modifications. The control_epoch is the harness’s control fence, which advances whenever a pause, cancellation, or interruption invalidates in-flight continuations, so an observation stamped with an older epoch cannot advance the current one. A distinct owner_epoch counts grants of trajectory ownership, so that storage can reject appends from a worker whose ownership has passed to another (the fencing mechanism of section 11.5.1). Neither ownership handoffs across workers nor control-epoch increments alter the unique logical identity of an in-flight operation.

↩︎ Epoch fencing, which keeps a stale asynchronous response from advancing the current continuation, is established in Transition guards and the admission check.

The software repair worksheet illustrates how these identifiers link distributed lifecycle events. Alongside the workspace snapshot ws_r18, the identifier operation_id=test_r18 designates the logical test suite invocation, while attempt_id=attempt1 tracks the specific network transport attempt. If an asynchronous test executor returns an acknowledgment containing a cluster job identifier such as job_tests_18, that handle correlates future asynchronous callbacks with the original operation. However, a recovering worker can discover this link only if that acknowledgment was durably flushed to the log before the crash. A durable ToolResult may record an active execution state, a timeout, or a completed test report; the reducer interprets these payloads according to their authenticated provenance. If an asynchronous execution finishes after the supervisor has already timed out the enclosing step, the late-arriving ToolResult updates the operation table rather than corrupting an obsolete execution branch.

↩︎ The observation-eligibility protocol that routes late results to the operation table rather than the active turn is established in Late results and observation eligibility.

Capturing model context requires equal diligence regarding immutability. The manifest in ContextBuilt must record the exact tokenized payload or byte stream delivered over the wire, rather than mutable file system paths or external URLs that could mutate before recovery occurs. The log must also capture available inference engine provenance (model checkpoints, serving system versions, and decoding configurations), recording any gaps because hosted model identifiers frequently hide upstream weight updates or inference engine modifications. When execution resumes, the replacement worker can assemble an updated prompt context from recent evidence while preserving the historical invocation records exactly as delivered.

Defining an event schema specifies what state reconstruction requires, but schema definitions alone cannot guarantee that writes reach disk before an agent issues network requests. If a runtime persists a model completion but crashes before flushing the subsequent tool intent, the recovering worker cannot determine whether an external tool call was dispatched, risking duplicate side effects. To resolve this vulnerability, the runtime must enforce strict write-ahead ordering between logging an operational intent and releasing its network dispatch.

11.3 Durable Intent Before Dispatch

In the crash worksheet, the repair worker’s next step is to submit test_r18 against workspace ws_r18. If the harness dispatches that test request across the network and crashes before the ToolIntent record reaches durable storage, a replacement worker folding the surviving log recovers a plan that still says “run the test.” Meanwhile the original job is already executing on the remote cluster and accruing charges. Recording only the eventual result leaves that blind spot open on every call. To prevent duplicate runs and untracked financial liability, the runtime must commit the admitted request and its reservation to persistent storage before that request ever leaves its control.

Recording the admitted request durably before dispatching it is classical write-ahead logging applied to trajectory dispatch. A durable history ensures that recovery can identify every external operation that may have begun, even if network partitions or crashes prevent the worker from ever receiving the reply. Under the runtime’s declared failure model, an external mutation that occurs without a recoverable record of intent is a phantom effect. Eliminating phantom effects requires two guarantees, strict write-ahead ordering in the runtime execution pipeline and a storage substrate that reliably outlives the failures the system claims to tolerate. Together they are the Part IV principle of intent before effect (principle 11), whose ordering this section derives (equation 1) and whose storage half it prices.

↪ The database form of write-ahead logging, and why a runtime borrows its ordering but not its control over remote state, is developed in Classical Transaction Background.

Definition 11.2: Durable intent

Durable intent is the immutable record of an admitted operation, capturing its identity, input arguments, a reference to its authorization decision and capability grant, and its budget reservation, acknowledged by storage that survives the declared failure domain before the request is dispatched.

  1. Significance: It converts a crash between dispatch and reply from an untracked phantom effect into an unresolved operation with an explicit owner, at the price of placing one durable acknowledgment on the critical path of every dispatch (table 2).
  2. Distinction: A durable intent records that work may have escaped into the external environment. It does not certify that the remote endpoint committed or executed the work, nor does it mandate that a recovered worker must re-execute it.
  3. Common pitfall: Taking an acknowledgment from storage that shares the failure boundary the design claims to survive, such as writing to a local scratch volume under a system contract that promises resilience against host or volume loss.

The intent references the grant rather than copying a secret into it. A log retained for months and read during replay is the wrong place for a credential, so the secret stays with the credential broker, and a recovered worker obtains a fresh use of it only by passing admission under current ownership.

↩︎ The credential broker, which holds a secret outside the guest and uses it on behalf of an authorized request, is established in Credential brokering.

11.3.1 Acknowledgment and the failure domain

Suppose the repair worker appends the test_r18 intent with a standard POSIX write() call, observes a successful return, and promptly dispatches the test. The host then suffers an immediate power failure. A successful write() guarantees only that the kernel copied the payload into its page cache; those dirty pages evaporate when the power cuts before writeback to disk. When the replacement worker boots, it finds an empty log, completely unaware that a remote test is already active. A returned write() therefore covers only the first failure domain of section 11.2, a process crash on an intact host, while offering zero protection against host power loss.

This reasoning extends upward through the failure hierarchy. If a worker fails, a replacement process can recover using reattachable block storage, provided that the replacement can mount the disk and read the acknowledged log and artifacts. If the failure domain includes the permanent destruction of the worker’s storage volume, as in cloud environments where spot instances or underlying hardware failures destroy ephemeral disks, the acknowledgment must originate from a storage service outside that volume. Remoteness alone does not satisfy this requirement. An asynchronous replica updated out of band can lag the primary log, leaving the replacement worker with an incomplete history during a failover.

Each step up the failure hierarchy demands a correspondingly stronger persistence barrier. Surviving host power loss requires fsync() or fdatasync(), which returns only after the underlying storage device reports that the data has reached stable media. The two system calls differ in how they handle metadata: fsync() flushes file attributes such as modification timestamps, whereas fdatasync() flushes only the metadata strictly required to read the payload back. For a trajectory log that preallocates its disk files, using fdatasync() eliminates an unneeded disk seek and metadata write per barrier. Even with these system calls, subtle failure modes remain.2 If a drive contains a volatile write cache, it must honor the disk flush command before an acknowledgment truly covers power loss. Alternatively, the write must specify Force Unit Access (a per-write flag that bypasses the drive cache entirely). Even a verified local barrier, however, cannot survive the catastrophic loss of the physical drive or volume. Surviving permanent volume loss requires a replicated storage engine that returns a success acknowledgment only after a quorum of replicas, distributed across distinct failure domains, have committed the record.3 The worksheet requires two durable copies outside the worker volume before dispatch. This chosen placement policy is stronger than the single-volume-loss requirement alone; table 2 lists acknowledgments that satisfy the stated contracts.

2 Crash Consistency in Practice: A study of eleven widely used applications, including databases, key-value stores, and version-control systems, found 60 crash vulnerabilities, because each application’s update protocol depended on subtle file-system persistence behavior that its authors had assumed rather than tested (Pillai et al. 2014). A trajectory log inherits the same exposure, so its durability claim should be checked by injecting crashes between every write and barrier, not inferred from the calls it makes.

Pillai, Thanumalayan Sankaranarayana, Vijay Chidambaram, Ramnatthan Alagappan, Samer Al-Kiswany, Andrea C. Arpaci-Dusseau, and Remzi H. Arpaci-Dusseau. 2014. “All File Systems Are Not Created Equal: On the Complexity of Crafting Crash-Consistent Applications.” 11th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI), 433–48.

3 Quorum Acknowledgment: Consensus protocols such as Paxos (Lamport 1998) and Raft (Ongaro and Ousterhout 2014) commit a log entry once a majority of replicas store it; because any two majorities share a replica, an acknowledged entry survives the loss of any minority. The guarantee covers only failures the replicas do not share, so a quorum placed on one host or one power domain gives the latency of replication without the durability the worksheet’s contract requires.

Lamport, Leslie. 1998. “The Part-Time Parliament.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 16 (2): 133–69. https://doi.org/10.1145/279227.279229.
Ongaro, Diego, and John Ousterhout. 2014. “In Search of an Understandable Consensus Algorithm.” Proceedings of the 2014 USENIX Annual Technical Conference (USENIX ATC 14), 305–19.

The implementation must define precisely which failure modes its persistence layer covers, how storage errors surface to the application, and how a replacement worker discovers the complete authoritative history. Underlying storage engines provide the mechanical primitives, but the systems architect must establish the durability contract. When an acknowledgment fails or times out with an ambiguous status, the runtime must hold dispatch and resolve the append according to the storage contract. Resolution means re-reading the authoritative log. If the intent is present, the attempt is admitted but still unsent, so a pause or a failed recheck can still withhold it. If a complete authoritative prefix lacks it, the worker appends it again under the same event identity, which storage deduplicates if the first append did land. Holding back a dispatch during a storage outage may temporarily stall progress, but dispatching before confirmation permanently destroys the evidence required to govern in-flight work after a crash.

↪ Why a durability claim must name both its acknowledgment and the failures it survives, and how durability differs from atomicity and isolation, is developed in Guarantee Boundaries.

11.3.2 Durable admission ordering

If a worker validates a proposal and reserves budget in memory (Transition guards and the admission check) but dispatches the network call before the storage acknowledgment returns, a crash in that gap leaves an untracked phantom effect. Durable execution closes that window by inserting an acknowledged intent write between admission and network release. For an attempt that proceeds to dispatch and later produces an observation, the ordering is

\[ \ValidatedProposalEvent \prec \AdmissionDecisionEvent \prec \IntentAcknowledgmentEvent \prec \ExternalDispatchAction \prec \EvidenceRecordEvent. \tag{1}\]

In equation 1, \(\ValidatedProposalEvent\) is the validated complete proposal, \(\AdmissionDecisionEvent\) is the admitted authorization and reservation decision, and \(\IntentAcknowledgmentEvent\) acknowledges its recoverable ToolIntent and required preceding records under the declared failure domain. The action \(\ExternalDispatchAction\) is dispatch, not endpoint completion. The event \(\EvidenceRecordEvent\) is the later durable recording of received evidence, whose status may still be pending, partial, or unknown. The ordering constrains the events that occur, though an attempt can stop before dispatch or never produce evidence. Its payoff is that the position of a crash in this sequence determines what recovery may conclude (figure 2). A crash before the worker receives \(\IntentAcknowledgmentEvent\) means the attempt was never dispatched, though an intent may already be durable if its acknowledgment was lost in transit. Recovery must inspect the complete authoritative prefix to determine whether that intent survived. Once \(\IntentAcknowledgmentEvent\) is durable, a crash at any point before adequate outcome evidence \(\EvidenceRecordEvent\) becomes durable leaves an in-doubt action, an admitted operation whose outcome the surviving evidence cannot settle. Because releasing the network request \(\ExternalDispatchAction\) does not write a separate log record, the surviving log looks identical whether the worker crashed just before sending the packet, while the request was in flight, or after recording a pending or timeout observation. That window is the tool layer’s uncertain outcome (definition 7.4) seen from the recovery side, and it is what the fold of section 11.2.1 marked unresolved. Only recorded, correlated evidence establishes an outcome.

In the crash worksheet, the intent names operation_id=test_r18, attempt_id=attempt1, the immutable ws_r18 input, the selected check, and the endpoint’s repeat contract. It references the applicable Authorized and BudgetReserved decisions, together with the requester and task bindings, control_epoch, and owner_epoch under which they were made. The recoverable prefix must reconstruct the admitted reservation itself, rather than merely a remaining balance.

Admission and control stay coordinated while the barrier is pending. An operator pause or an administrative change in authority can still intercept and withhold an admitted but unsent request, because logging a durable intent records permission to dispatch rather than an irrevocable obligation to execute. The harness’s dispatch-time recheck, the last gate before the request leaves, re-evaluates the admitted attempt against current control state without reserving a second batch of budget. If the release mechanism already dispatched the request across the network, subsequent control actions must treat the request as sent. Conversely, a withheld request keeps its entry in the operation table and its reserved budget until verified evidence confirms that no remote side effects or financial liabilities remain.

↩︎ Dispatch-time rechecks, which re-run budget, authority, and approval checks at the moment of release, are established in Dispatch-time rechecks.

Foundation model invocations require identical rigor regarding identity and liability. Token generation streams over network sockets, and an interrupted invocation can incur charges on the provider’s bill even if a worker crash prevents the client from ever logging the output. The same ordering therefore covers model calls. The ContextBuilt record and the invocation’s BudgetReserved decision become durable before the request leaves, so a crash mid-stream leaves a logged invocation with no ModelReplied. Recovery treats that invocation as in doubt only for its charge, which it settles from the provider’s usage evidence. The lost reply cannot be reconstructed by replaying the prompt, because stochastic sampling produces a different completion, so recovery discards any partial stream and never replays the call. Any retry is a separately admitted invocation, requiring a fresh invocation identity, validated request and context, current admission checks, and a durable budget reservation before dispatch.

11.3.3 Recovery evidence and the completeness rule

The first of the three recovery states in figure 2 (not dispatched) carries a hidden precondition. Following a crash, a recovering worker can treat the absence of a logged intent as proof that dispatch never occurred only if it holds the complete authoritative history promised by the failure domain. This requirement is the completeness rule. An asynchronously updated read replica cannot satisfy this rule, because the replica cannot distinguish between an operation that was never dispatched and an intent whose log record has not yet arrived across the network. Table 3 outlines the recovery states governed by this rule.

In the crash worksheet scenario, the dispatched test may have failed to reach the service, may still be executing on a remote runner, or may have finished just before the worker crashed. If the executor’s reply accepting the job was never logged, its job handle, job_tests_18, is lost with the worker as well. To resolve this ambiguity, the runtime performs a targeted lookup using the logged operation identity, authenticating and correlating any newly discovered evidence before updating the operation table. A verified test result establishes the check for ws_r18; whether to accept the overall repair remains a distinct policy decision.

The in-doubt status reflects runtime uncertainty rather than external failure, and resolving it is a question about the endpoint. Operation identity and endpoint contracts details the endpoint requirements needed to execute a safe repeat: strict identity scoping, immutable payload binding, retention of prior execution results, handling of concurrent in-flight requests, and coordination between state mutations and returned values. A client-provided idempotency key protects only those endpoints that implement these semantics. Probing an endpoint by resource name provides much weaker guarantees, as a matching name might belong to an unrelated operation, while an absent resource could indicate that a delayed initial request is still in transit. For an in-doubt action, durable execution therefore applies settlement before retry (principle 8) unchanged. The log supplies the operation identity and its evidence, and only the endpoint’s contract or positive outcome evidence licenses another attempt.

↪ The idempotence and deduplication contracts behind a safe repeat are developed in Repetition and Result Recovery.

Systems Perspective 11.1: Write-ahead logging, from pages to endpoints
Database write-ahead logging forces a modification’s log record to stable storage before the corresponding dirty page can be written to disk, ensuring that crash recovery can redo committed updates and undo uncommitted ones (Gray and Reuter 1992; Mohan et al. 1992). This dual guarantee works because the database engine exerts exclusive ownership over its buffer pool and disk pages. In contrast, an agent runtime logs intent for side effects that occur at remote endpoints outside its administrative control. The runtime log can reconstruct the internal state of the agent, but it holds no undo records for external systems, and external tool endpoints rarely expose a two-phase commit prepare phase to tentatively hold modifications (Gray 1978). A durable intent is therefore a unilateral record that work may have escaped, with no prepared state at the endpoint that recovery could commit or roll back. Distributed systems architects reached an identical conclusion when scaling web architectures, replacing heavyweight distributed transactions with idempotent messaging across independent administrative domains (Helland 2007). The logging protocol applies directly; what changes is the recovery model, which must explicitly handle in-doubt operations whenever endpoint evidence is missing.

When surviving evidence proves insufficient to resolve an action, the operation table keeps an owner assigned for reconciliation or explicit operator intervention across timeouts, pauses, and terminal execution paths. Independent branches of execution can proceed under their own guards while dependent operations await resolution. Remediating an effect that is established but unwanted calls for a compensating action, a follow-up operation that undoes or offsets it, which The Trajectory Saga Pattern develops.

11.3.4 Group commit

Intent before effect puts one durable acknowledgment on the critical path of every dispatch. For a single trajectory the price is small, because a millisecond-scale acknowledgment sits beside a turn that spends seconds on model and tool work. The price that matters is throughput. An agent runtime managing dozens of concurrent trajectories pays that barrier separately for each dispatch, and every dispatch in the fleet queues for the same storage service. Under the worksheet’s replicated quorum contract, each barrier is a network round trip to remote storage nodes. Group commit4 mitigates this overhead by coalescing multiple ready log records into a single shared storage acknowledgment (DeWitt et al. 1984). A batch coordinator aggregates pending intent records, issues one consolidated write to the replicated store, and unblocks individual dispatches only after receiving a successful quorum acknowledgment. Even within a batch, every individual trajectory strictly maintains write-ahead intent ordering before transmission.

4 Group Commit Origin: DeWitt and colleagues introduced group commit for main-memory database systems, where memory transactions executed so quickly that the physical log write at each commit became the primary throughput bottleneck (DeWitt et al. 1984). Agent dispatch enters the same regime whenever durable intent logging requires a network round trip to a storage quorum, allowing classical database batching techniques to apply directly to trajectory logging.

DeWitt, David J., Randy H. Katz, Frank Olken, Leonard D. Shapiro, Michael R. Stonebraker, and David A. Wood. 1984. “Implementation Techniques for Main Memory Database Systems.” Proceedings of the 1984 ACM SIGMOD International Conference on Management of Data, 1–8.

Every batch member waits the whole shared flush; only storage work divides.

For a successful batch, let \(\QueueArrivalTime\) be the time event \(\BatchedEventIndex\) enters the batching queue, \(\BatchFlushStartTime\) the start of the batch’s storage operation, and \(\DurableAcknowledgmentTime\) the time its durable acknowledgment becomes available. If notification delay is excluded, event \(\BatchedEventIndex\) waits

\[ \GroupCommitWaitTime = (\BatchFlushStartTime-\QueueArrivalTime) + (\DurableAcknowledgmentTime-\BatchFlushStartTime) = \DurableAcknowledgmentTime-\QueueArrivalTime. \tag{2}\]

The first term in equation 2 captures queueing and batch aggregation delay, while the second term represents the shared physical storage latency. If a batch contains \(\CommitBatchRecordCount\) records, dividing the storage duration equally yields an amortized storage cost of \((\DurableAcknowledgmentTime-\BatchFlushStartTime)/\CommitBatchRecordCount\) per event. Amortization, however, does not compress elapsed time. Every member in the batch must wait through the full storage round trip, plus internal scheduling overhead, before its request can be dispatched.

Increasing the batch size reduces storage overhead per record but increases the queueing delay experienced by early arrivals. Under light traffic, aggregation delay dominates, making a batch flush timer essential to bound tail latency. Under heavy load, throughput amortization keeps queue depths manageable, though very large payloads can eventually inflate the storage operation itself. With a 2 ms timer and a 4 ms quorum acknowledgment, a batch of 16 intents incurs only 0.25 ms of amortized storage work per intent. Yet the first arrival waits 6 ms, and even the final arrival still waits the full 4 ms storage round trip. A sound design therefore sizes the batch timer within the allowable dispatch latency budget, instruments queueing delay separately from storage service time, and never weakens the declared durability domain to shave milliseconds off dispatch latency.

Checkpoint 11.1: Intent, evidence, and the durability domain

Write-ahead logging protects the identity and liabilities of actions that escape the runtime, but an event history alone cannot reconstruct the state of mutated files, code repositories, or training environments. Each log boundary must therefore be coupled to a verified snapshot of the surrounding workspace, so that a recovering worker restores the precise execution context in which those actions were taken.

11.4 Checkpoints

A durable intent informs a replacement worker that an operation such as the r18 test may have begun, but it does not store the files that the test actually touched. An execution log stating that an edit succeeded cannot restore modified bytes unless those bytes, or reconstructible deltas against a known base, remain preserved in storage. Durable execution therefore requires the control record and the mutable workspace to share a common recovery boundary.

A checkpoint binds a recoverable record snapshot, its associated workspace files, and all required external artifacts to an exact, committed log position. Checkpointing accelerates recovery by replacing an unbounded prefix of historical events with a single compatible snapshot. This architectural checkpoint differs from the compact progress summary used to assemble prompt context: an LLM prompt summary cannot reconstruct raw filesystem bytes, prove resource liabilities, or track uncommitted external operations.

11.4.1 Consistent recovery boundaries

If a runtime snapshots the control record at one instant and copies the workspace directory a few seconds later while a tool is writing files, restoring the pair produces a torn state. In a torn state, the record refers to file bytes that the snapshot lacks, or the reverse.5 A checkpoint must therefore freeze both halves at an authoritative log position \(\CheckpointLogPosition\), mirroring the fold boundary \(\LogPositionIndex\), rather than at a loose model turn. A checkpoint manifest serves as the formal contract for this boundary. It binds \(\CheckpointLogPosition\) to the record snapshot, the workspace snapshot, references to external artifacts, cryptographic hashes for integrity, and the reducer schema versions required to interpret them. The control record snapshot captures cumulative resource consumption, active budget reservations, approval states, and in-flight operations with their identities and settlement owners. These obligations persist in the record even while a crashed agent awaits replacement.

5 Consistent Snapshot: Chandy and Lamport defined a consistent global state of a distributed system as one in which every recorded message receipt has a recorded send, and gave a protocol that captures such a state without stopping the system (Chandy and Lamport 1985). A trajectory checkpoint obeys the same test on a smaller scale: the record must not refer to workspace bytes that the paired snapshot lacks, or restoration produces a state that never existed.

Chandy, K. Mani, and Leslie Lamport. 1985. “Distributed Snapshots: Determining Global States of Distributed Systems.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 3 (1): 63–75. https://doi.org/10.1145/214451.214456.

Two capture designs keep the record and the filesystem consistent at \(\CheckpointLogPosition\). One design pauses workspace mutations, drains in-flight disk writes, and captures the record and filesystem together at a quiet boundary. An alternative design stores workspace states as content-addressed, immutable trees, each named by a hash of its bytes (section 11.8.2), and maps each versioned root directly to a log boundary. External tasks may remain in flight during capture under either design, provided the checkpoint explicitly records their unresolved status and idempotency tokens.

Snapshot publication must strictly follow data availability. The runtime first captures the consistent state and flushes all underlying bytes to durable storage within the failure domain. Only after those bytes are durable does the runtime commit the manifest or append a Checkpoint event to the authoritative log, marking the snapshot eligible for recovery. If a host crashes during capture, the recovery supervisor ignores the incomplete snapshot and falls back to the previous published checkpoint and its subsequent log suffix. Background cleanup can safely reclaim unreferenced staging files, provided garbage collection protects any artifacts still referenced by active recovery paths. Operating systems and storage engines frequently offer copy-on-write snapshots (file-system subvolume snapshots or process-level memory forks) to freeze point-in-time state with near-zero latency; however, serializing and offloading those blocks to independent durable storage remains a necessary second phase.

The publication order in figure 3 makes the recovery boundary explicit. A compatible pair becomes eligible only after its payload bytes are durable and its manifest has entered the authoritative history.

An unpublished capture does not advance the recovery boundary, even if some of its files survived the crash. Likewise, a published control position cannot substitute for a missing workspace payload. Recovery needs both the committed reference and the bytes it identifies.

Restoration proceeds deterministically. The replacement supervisor first verifies the integrity hashes in the manifest, ensures all referenced artifacts are accessible, and fetches the authoritative log suffix spanning from \(\CheckpointLogPosition\) to the desired recovery target \(\RecoveryTargetLogPosition\). It unpacks the record and workspace snapshots at \(\CheckpointLogPosition\), replays the intervening control events through the state reducer, and applies recorded workspace patches up to \(\RecoveryTargetLogPosition\). This reconstruction step invokes neither the foundation model nor the original tool implementations. Instead, it writes preserved byte streams into the worker’s isolated workspace, reinstating identical state without re-executing nondeterministic actions.

Consider the crash worksheet scenario: the checkpoint at \(\CheckpointLogPosition\) binds the record snapshot record_R_ckpt to workspace snapshot ws_r18. The subsequent log suffix captures the model prompt and response, token budget reservations, and the durable intent for operation test_r18 under attempt1. Suppose the host crashes while the test executes, before any execution result reaches disk. Because the test executed against an immutable snapshot using isolated scratch storage, the main candidate workspace experienced zero mutations during this suffix. To recover, the replacement worker restores ws_r18 and folds the suffix events. This reconstructs the pending test obligation and its outstanding budget reservation, recognizing that the operation remains in doubt. Any intermediate scratch data or uncommitted output artifacts must either possess durable references or be marked unavailable.

When an execution suffix modifies workspace files, durable recovery requires comprehensive delta tracking: the base snapshot identifier, ordered byte replacements or unified diffs, file deletions, metadata flags, and SHA-256 integrity checksums. A success return code or a dangling content hash whose backing payload was lost to a crash cannot reconstruct state. If the control log points to a workspace revision whose delta payload is missing from storage, the runtime must fail fast and flag the state as unrecoverable, rather than erroneously pairing a forward log position with an obsolete directory tree. Recomputing missing workspace artifacts constitutes new nondeterministic execution, which falls under admission and repair policy rather than reconstruction.

↪ Whether to repair a failed step or rebuild its state, and the admission check every recovery action must pass first, are developed in Forward Recovery.

11.4.2 Full and incremental snapshots

As an agent writes workspace files, adds operation-table entries, and retains observations turn by turn, copying the entire state at every checkpoint rewrites every previously saved byte. Architects can avoid that repeated copying by storing incremental delta chains instead of full snapshots, satisfying the same recovery contract while trading storage write bandwidth against reconstruction latency. Under full snapshots, restoration latency scales with total state size; under incremental deltas, it scales with the length of the delta chain.

Write volume separates the two representations when checkpointed state accumulates monotonically. What grows across turns is not the log itself, which already appends each turn’s events once, but the state those events produced. A full snapshot re-serializes all of that accumulated state at every checkpoint. A delta, in contrast, holds only the workspace bytes and record fields that changed since the previous checkpoint, which is a different object from the event suffix the reducer folds. Suppose state grows by \(\TurnStateGrowth\) bytes per turn and the runtime writes a checkpoint every \(\CheckpointStride\) turns. The \(\CheckpointOrdinalIndex\)th full snapshot must re-serialize all \(\CheckpointOrdinalIndex \cdot \TurnStateGrowth \cdot \CheckpointStride\) accumulated bytes. Over a trajectory requiring \(\TrajectoryCheckpointCount\) checkpoints, the cumulative write volumes are:

\[\FullSnapshotWriteVolume = \sum_{\CheckpointOrdinalIndex=1}^{\TrajectoryCheckpointCount} \CheckpointOrdinalIndex\,\TurnStateGrowth\,\CheckpointStride = \TurnStateGrowth\,\CheckpointStride\,\frac{\TrajectoryCheckpointCount(\TrajectoryCheckpointCount+1)}{2}, \qquad \IncrementalSnapshotWriteVolume = \TurnStateGrowth\,\CheckpointStride\,\TrajectoryCheckpointCount. \tag{3}\]

As equation 3 illustrates, full snapshots cause write volume to scale quadratically with trajectory length, whereas incremental deltas scale linearly. The ratio between them, \((\TrajectoryCheckpointCount+1)/2\), represents the write amplification penalty incurred by full snapshots, which spend storage I/O by re-serializing previously checkpointed state at every boundary.

Full snapshots rewrite old state, so their bytes grow quadratically.

For a 500-turn trajectory whose state expands by 128 KB per turn, checkpointing every 5 turns produces 100 checkpoints. Full snapshots write 3.2 GB over the run, whereas incremental deltas write only 64 MB, a 50.5-fold reduction in write volume. The saving moves cost onto the recovery path. Instead of loading a single flat image, restoration must fetch, validate, and replay the sequential delta chain, so the choice turns on what each representation needs to survive and which capture and reconstruction costs must be measured (table 4).

Periodic consolidation can merge an extended delta chain back into a new base snapshot, but the garbage collector must verify that the new base is durably published before retiring earlier deltas. Snapshot retention policies also hinge on audit requirements: while restoring an active agent only requires the latest base and its subsequent deltas, compliance or post-hoc debugging may mandate retaining older historical snapshots across the entire trajectory.

11.4.3 Checkpoint cadence

Checkpointing after every turn pauses the trajectory on every step to copy state that the log already recorded, whereas never checkpointing forces a recovering worker to replay an entire run’s suffix or recompute unlogged work from scratch. Selecting a checkpoint cadence balances that snapshot pause against the time required to reconstruct the suffix after a crash. When logged events and workspace deltas survive host failure, replaying that suffix incurs only the modest cost of reading, validating, folding, and applying recorded mutations, with no foundation-model inference. The agent resumes dispatching new model calls only after state reconstruction and guarded resume succeed.

Classical checkpointing models address a harsher failure regime: one where a crash obliterates all in-flight work, forcing the system to recompute from the last checkpoint.6 In agentic execution, this regime governs state the log does not capture. A long local analysis step that keeps intermediate results in memory, or workspace writes that bypass the write-ahead log, must be recomputed from the last checkpoint after a crash. An in-flight tool or model call is a different case that no cadence can help, because recovery treats it as in doubt rather than as lost work. Under Poisson failure assumptions, each crash discards an average of half a checkpoint interval of execution. Balancing that expected loss against the pause overhead of writing snapshots determines the classical checkpoint interval.

6 Checkpoint Interval Lineage: Young posed the problem for long batch jobs that saved restart information periodically so that a random failure need not restart the job from the beginning (Young 1974). The derivation assumes that lost work must be computed again; a trajectory whose turns are logged violates that assumption, which is why the interval lengthens once the log absorbs the lost-work term.

Young, John W. 1974. “A First Order Approximation to the Optimum Checkpoint Interval.” Communications of the ACM 17 (9): 530–31. https://doi.org/10.1145/361147.361115.
Theorem 11.1: Optimal checkpoint interval (Young's approximation)

A checkpoint taken too often wastes pauses; one taken too rarely wastes re-execution after each failure. Assume that failures are rare, Poisson, and independent of progress, that each failure loses on average half an interval of useful work, and that the pause is small relative to the interval. Under these assumptions, Young’s approximation (Young 1974) gives the useful-work interval between checkpoints that minimizes expected overhead:

\[\OptimalCheckpointInterval \approx \sqrt{2\,\CheckpointWriteTime\,\MeanTimeBetweenFailures} \tag{4}\]

  • Checkpoint write time (\(\CheckpointWriteTime\)): Time execution stops to capture and durably publish one checkpoint.
  • Mean time between failures (\(\MeanTimeBetweenFailures\)): Expected time between crashes that lose unrecorded work.
  • Overhead at the optimum: The cadence-dependent overhead is \(\sqrt{2\,\CheckpointWriteTime/\MeanTimeBetweenFailures}\) of useful time. Checkpoint lost-work balance derives the interval, from which this overhead follows, and Daly (2006) refines both for failures during saving and recovery.
  • Sensitivity: For a 2 s pause and a mean time between failures of 10 minutes, \(\OptimalCheckpointInterval \approx\) 49 s and the overhead is 8.2 percent. The minimum is flat: an interval off by a factor of 2 in either direction raises overhead by only 25 percent, while a factor-of-10 miss raises it to 41 percent.
Daly, J. T. 2006. “A Higher Order Estimate of the Optimum Checkpoint Interval for Restart Dumps.” Future Generation Computer Systems 22 (3): 303–12. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.future.2004.11.016.

7 Determinants: Log-based recovery protocols record each nondeterministic event as a determinant, the information needed to reproduce its outcome during recovery, and are classified by when determinants reach stable storage (Elnozahy et al. 2002). In an agent trajectory the model reply is the dominant determinant: a sampled reply cannot be regenerated, so logging it is what lets the fold replace re-execution.

Elnozahy, Elmootazbellah N., Lorenzo Alvisi, Yi-Min Wang, and David B. Johnson. 2002. “A Survey of Rollback-Recovery Protocols in Message-Passing Systems.” ACM Computing Surveys 34 (3): 375–408. https://doi.org/10.1145/568522.568525.

Appending events to a persistent trajectory log alters this trade-off by reducing the lost-work penalty without changing the snapshot pause. In distributed systems literature, pairing periodic snapshots with an append-only log of nondeterministic inputs is termed log-based rollback recovery (Elnozahy et al. 2002).7 When an agent durably records model outputs and workspace diffs turn by turn, recovery costs only the time to replay and validate those entries rather than re-executing inference. Substituting this smaller reconstruction cost into the same balance lengthens the best interval, as the derivation below shows. Only work whose state the log does not capture, such as unlogged workspace writes or in-memory analysis, still pays the full re-execution penalty that equation 4 prices.

Overhead is flat near the optimum interval and steep far from it.

Counted in turns rather than seconds, the logged balance has the same shape as Young’s. Let a checkpoint every \(\CheckpointStride\) turns pause execution for \(\CheckpointWriteTime\), as in equation 4. Let crashes arrive at failure rate \(\TurnFailureRate\) per completed turn. Let \(\TurnReconstructionTime\) be the time to reconstruct one logged turn of the suffix (reading, validating, folding, and applying its records). Per completed turn, checkpointing costs \(\CheckpointWriteTime/\CheckpointStride\). A crash finds on average \(\CheckpointStride/2\) turns past the last checkpoint, so expected reconstruction costs \(\TurnFailureRate\,\TurnReconstructionTime\,\CheckpointStride/2\) per turn. Setting the derivative of the sum to zero gives the logged stride

\[\CheckpointStride^* = \sqrt{\frac{2\,\CheckpointWriteTime}{\TurnFailureRate\,\TurnReconstructionTime}}. \tag{5}\]

Repeating the derivation with \(\TurnReexecutionTime\), the cost of re-executing an unlogged turn’s model calls and tools, in place of \(\TurnReconstructionTime\) recovers Young’s regime. Logging therefore lengthens the best stride by \(\sqrt{\TurnReexecutionTime/\TurnReconstructionTime}\). The cost ratio itself is large, because a re-executed turn samples the model again while a reconstructed one only reads records, but the stride grows only with its square root.

An averaged balance hides how a particular failure trace spends its time, so a design is checked against traces as well. Let \(\StrideCheckpointCount_k\) denote the number of checkpoints taken at stride \(\CheckpointStride\), \(\TraceCrashCount\) the number of crashes in the trace, and \(\CheckpointRestoreTime\) the fixed restore latency per crash. If crash \(\TraceCrashIndex\) leaves \(\LostSuffixTurnCount(\CheckpointStride)\) completed turns beyond the most recent checkpoint, the total time lost to snapshotting and recovery is

\[\CheckpointRecoveryOverheadTime(\CheckpointStride) = \StrideCheckpointCount_k \CheckpointWriteTime + \TraceCrashCount \CheckpointRestoreTime + \TurnReconstructionTime\sum_i \LostSuffixTurnCount(\CheckpointStride), \tag{6}\]

with every term in seconds. Charging reconstruction per turn simplifies the model, though in production one turn’s suffix may span several fine-grained log records.

Napkin Math 11.1: Checkpoint cadence on a fixed crash trace
Problem: A hypothetical trajectory completes 100 turns, with crashes after turns 37 and 83. Each published checkpoint pauses execution for 0.5 s. Each crash costs 1 s to restore the checkpoint plus 0.1 s per turn of recorded events and workspace changes since it. An initial checkpoint exists, every required suffix survives, and each policy also checkpoints at the final turn; only capture and reconstruction time count. Which stride, 10, 25, or 50 turns, spends the least time on this trace?

Math: The 10-turn policy publishes 10 checkpoints and reconstructs suffixes of 7 and 3 turns. The 25-turn policy publishes 4 checkpoints and reconstructs suffixes of 12 and 8 turns. The 50-turn policy publishes 2 checkpoints and reconstructs suffixes of 37 and 33 turns. Reconstruction totals are 3 s, 4 s, and 9 s; adding capture pauses gives \(\CheckpointRecoveryOverheadTime(\CheckpointStride)\) of 8 s, 6 s, and 10 s (equation 6). The trace’s failure rate is \(\TurnFailureRate =\) 0.02 crashes per turn, so the logged balance (equation 5) gives \(\CheckpointStride^* = \sqrt{2\CheckpointWriteTime/(\TurnFailureRate \TurnReconstructionTime)} \approx\) 22 turns. If each lost turn instead had to be re-executed at \(\TurnReexecutionTime\) = 20 s, the same balance gives \(\CheckpointStride^* \approx\) 1.6 turns.

Result: The 25-turn stride wins on this trace, at 6 s, next to the balance point of about 22 turns.

Systems insight: Logging each turn’s events and workspace changes lengthens the best stride by \(\sqrt{\TurnReexecutionTime/\TurnReconstructionTime}\), about 14.1× here, because a crash then costs reconstruction instead of repeated model calls. The stride moves with the square root of each knob: quadrupling the capture pause doubles it, and so does quartering the crash rate. Because the optimum is flat (theorem 11.1), a stride within a factor of 2 of the balance point costs at most 25 percent extra overhead. Measure \(\CheckpointWriteTime\), \(\TurnReconstructionTime\), and the crash rate from fault-injection traces before tuning more finely.

Operational transitions such as waiting for human approval, compacting execution context, or performing a zero-downtime worker handoff justify taking an opportunistic, out-of-cadence checkpoint if doing so truncates pending recovery chains or enables log pruning. If the record and workspace artifacts are already durable and recoverable, these boundary events require no extra snapshot. Checkpoint cadence remains an efficiency trade-off, whereas intent ordering is an absolute correctness invariant that no cadence optimization can relax.

Checkpoint 11.2: A checkpoint must match its suffix

A checkpoint and its suffix bring the worker’s internal record and workspace state up to date, but recovery does not automatically grant authority to resume external execution. The replacement worker must first establish exclusive lease ownership, reconcile pending liabilities, and verify fencing tokens before dispatching new external side effects.

11.5 Resuming a Trajectory

The worksheet’s replacement worker now holds everything the previous sections promised: the checkpoint that binds record_R_ckpt to ws_r18, and a suffix that ends at the durable intent for test_r18 under attempt1 with no result. Holding that record is not yet permission to act on it. The workspace can be restored byte for byte, but the missing result supports no inference about the test, which may never have launched, may have finished as the host lost power, or may still be running remotely. Another worker may also believe it still owns the trajectory, and the budget and approvals recorded before the crash may no longer match current policy. The replacement therefore cannot simply re-run the command or assume failure; it must establish ownership, restore state, settle what it can of the outstanding obligations, and only then admit new work.

Resume establishes a usable continuation from surviving records under current ownership and cluster policy. It proceeds in five stages, which the subsections below follow in order. The worker validates that the authoritative log is available and acquires exclusive trajectory ownership. It then restores compatible in-memory records and workspace artifacts and reconciles outstanding external operations and resource budgets. Finally, it evaluates admission for new work, rebuilding serving state if that work begins with a model call. Reconstruction reproduces the agent’s causal history, while subsequent admission gates decide whether to grant or withhold execution authority. If required evidence, ownership fencing, or admission grants are unavailable, the runtime safely holds the trajectory in place rather than proceeding on speculative assumptions.

11.5.1 Availability and ownership

If a replacement worker starts reconstructing state from a lagging storage replica or begins dispatching while a network-partitioned predecessor is still running, it can miss an already-sent test_r18 intent or race against the old worker’s late writes. Before parsing records, the worker must therefore locate the authoritative committed log prefix and the required checkpoint artifacts. Under the log completeness rules of section 11.3, the absence of an entry is meaningful only after the worker proves that the log prefix is intact. If storage replicas disagree, network partitions obscure the tail, or an immutable artifact is missing from object storage, the worker must report that reconstruction is unavailable rather than treating the gap as an empty history.

Reading durable state does not confer the authority to act on it. Trajectory ownership requires an explicit grant from an ownership coordinator, a strongly consistent lease service whose own state is replicated so that it survives the failures the trajectory log survives. The coordinator authenticates the claimant and issues a new owner_epoch. This epoch bump assigns execution responsibility to the replacement worker while leaving record_version, control_epoch, and the identities of admitted operations unchanged.

The distinction between active ownership and historical inspection matters because the previous worker may have been partitioned rather than permanently destroyed. The tool layer met the same zombie worker, a stalled holder that outlives its lease and resumes writing, and the same remedy protects the whole trajectory. Ownership is held under a lease, so expiry bounds how long a lost worker can block recovery, but expiry alone is not exclusion. Exclusion requires a fencing token, a monotonically increasing ownership generation that stateful resources compare against the newest generation they have observed, rejecting requests stamped with stale epochs (Kleppmann 2016). For a trajectory the owner_epoch is that token. Fencing exclusion holds only at the boundaries that verify it: the write-ahead log, the dispatch gate, and each participating downstream resource. The replacement worker must install the new ownership generation at those boundaries before relying on mutual exclusion, and any boundary unable to verify current ownership must withhold protected operations.

↩︎ Leases and epoch fencing at the tool endpoint, with their lock-service origins, are established in Operation identity and endpoint contracts.

↪ The handoff in which the old owner’s delayed write meets the newly installed fence is developed in Leases and Fencing.

Fencing operates over a narrower scope than effect settlement. A dispatch gateway can reject new dispatch attempts from the old worker while an operation that the old worker previously initiated remains in flight on an external runner. The replacement worker therefore preserves operation_id=test_r18 along with the evidence recorded for that earlier attempt. Passing the ownership check authorizes the new worker to issue fresh dispatches, but it does not permit re-dispatching test_r18. A gateway’s local pending-or-complete tracking table cannot make its log record atomic with an independent service endpoint’s real-world side effects.

11.5.2 State restoration

Once ownership is secured, re-running past turns to rebuild memory would re-sample the model and risk repeating external side effects. Instead, the worker restores the control record and the workspace by the procedure of section 11.4.1, folding the committed suffix over the base checkpoint without calling any model or tool. For the worksheet, the result is the state already traced there, with ws_r18 untouched and the in-doubt intent and reservation for test_r18 in the record. What restoration cannot supply is the executor’s job handle job_tests_18, which survives only if the failed worker flushed the executor’s acknowledgment to the log before crashing. Without it, reconciliation must find the job by its logged operation identity.

11.5.3 Effect and charge reconciliation

Restoring the log leaves test_r18 in doubt, and the remote test runner may have finished and reported its result while no worker was alive to receive it. To keep such callbacks from vanishing with the crashed process, durable execution moves the harness’s result wakeup registrations out of worker memory into a durable result inbox that operates independently of any single host. The inbox retains authenticated completion records, indexed by trajectory identifier, logical operation, and attempt identity, and stamped with executor signatures and scope metadata for independent validation. The current trajectory owner ingests this evidence into the log and the operation_table. However, receiving an inbox message does not settle an operation. Pending statuses, gateway timeouts, and partial payloads leave the operation in doubt.

↩︎ The result wakeup pipeline and its register-before-send rule are established in The result wakeup pipeline.

When the inbox holds no settling record for test_r18, the worker queries the tool endpoint directly to recover the job and its execution output by reconciliation, a bounded status query by logical operation identity under the tool’s contract. If the endpoint returns a verified completion record, that result confirms the status of ws_r18 for that specific test run, though it does not imply that the broader repair objective has been met. If the endpoint cannot be reached or returns an ambiguous status, the operation remains in doubt. In that situation, the endpoint retry contract, rather than the absence of a response or a matching identifier, governs whether launching another attempt is safe.

↩︎ Reconciliation, the bounded inspection of external state that settles an ambiguous operation, is established in Reconciliation.

The same rigorous accounting applies to financial and computational budgets. The worker reconstructs actual_consumption, outstanding_reservations, and the protected finalization_reserve by replaying all recorded BudgetReserved and BudgetSettled transitions. External model providers and compute clusters do not refund resources because an orchestrator crashed mid-flight. A partial usage report settles only the verified fraction of a call, while the unverified remainder stays locked in outstanding reservations. The replacement worker inherits the duty to retrieve final usage evidence. Until that evidence arrives, the unverified remainder stays reserved as liability, the harness’s rule that missing usage is never released as unspent budget.

Control transitions and human approvals remain authoritative across failures. A trajectory recovered in the PAUSED state remains suspended, and a trajectory that reached a terminal continuation remains terminal. A held action recovers into AWAITING_APPROVAL, the state in which approval and dispatch eligibility are both unresolved. A pending request keeps waiting for its reviewer. An approval granted before the crash still releases the action only when the dispatch-time rechecks pass under the new owner. An approval that has expired, become stale, or been explicitly revoked remains void across all workers. When authenticated results arrive after an operation has timed out or the trajectory has paused, they update the operation_table to keep an accurate audit trail, rather than retroactively mutating an obsolete conversation turn. The current owner must validate those newly settled facts before admitting them into subsequent model contexts.

11.5.4 Continuation admission

During the minutes a trajectory spent down and recovering, its operating deadline may have expired, an operator may have paused the run, or an in-doubt test_r18 may still hold budget and block dependent edits. Recovered history accounts for past decisions, but current conditions govern the next step. Before admitting new work, the harness evaluates task and plan dependencies, capability grants, approval statuses, cluster dispatch capacity, and remaining funds. To pass admission, the sum of actual_consumption, outstanding_reservations, the bounded liability of the proposed step, and the protected finalization_reserve must fit within the cumulative budget ceiling. Deadlines for normal operation are enforced independently: when an operating deadline expires, the agent cannot schedule new exploratory calls simply because monetary budget remains. Instead, the runtime transitions strictly to finalization routines using the declared time and resource reserve.

↩︎ Bounded-liability admission, the budget rule this check enforces, is established in Reservation and settlement.

Unsettled external effects block dependent operations rather than stalling the entire system. An independent observation or read-only query may proceed if its own authorization and admission checks pass, whereas any action that mutates or depends on an in-doubt operation remains withheld. Resuming execution from PAUSED requires passing the guarded state machine transitions. Similarly, recovered partial model streams are discarded rather than promoted to completed candidates. A logged proposal that was never admitted requires fresh admission; an already admitted but definitely unsent attempt retains its identity and reservation and must pass the dispatch-time recheck without reserving budget again.

↩︎ The control-plane state machine and its guarded transitions out of PAUSED are established in Trajectory States.

Context assembly must distinguish historical reconstruction from forward continuation. When auditing earlier turns, the runtime inspects the immutable delivered-input manifest and recorded summaries that the model actually received. When constructing the prompt for the next turn (ContextBuilt), the prompt composer may incorporate newly settled test results or approved plan revisions. Systems must preserve historical input manifests verbatim rather than silently substituting fresh retrievals or recomputed summaries.

Infrastructure and prompt updates also demand explicit governance. During extended downtimes, operators may update model checkpoints, prompt templates, or tool schemas. The runtime can either pin execution to the recorded versions, validate a verified migration path to the updated schema, or halt continuation if semantic compatibility cannot be proven. An in-doubt intent preserves its original parameter semantics regardless of subsequent schema versions. Whenever configuration shifts occur, the runtime records the transition and its provenance boundaries so that future evaluations treat the executions as distinct configurations.

Dispatching a new model call requires compatible serving state, though not necessarily a cold start. Depending on cluster cache eviction policies and prefix tree management, the inference engine may retain the full KV prefix, retain reusable prefix segments, or require full context prefill. Inspecting an audit record or keeping a trajectory paused requires no prefill computation. A serial-stage estimate of resume latency sums the constituent stages:

\[\ResumeTime = \OwnershipAcquisitionTime + \CheckpointSuffixReadTime + \SuffixFoldTime + \WorkspaceRestoreTime + \OperationReconciliationTime + \PrefillTime, \tag{7}\]

which accounts for acquiring the ownership fence, fetching and verifying the checkpoint and suffix, folding the log suffix, restoring workspace files, reconciling outstanding external operations, and rebuilding serving state for the next invocation. When stages overlap, elapsed resume time follows their dependency critical path rather than this serial sum. The terms map onto the subsections in order. \(\OwnershipAcquisitionTime\) and \(\CheckpointSuffixReadTime\) belong to availability and ownership (the two can overlap, since reading the log needs no ownership), \(\SuffixFoldTime\) and \(\WorkspaceRestoreTime\) to state restoration, \(\OperationReconciliationTime\) to reconciliation, and \(\PrefillTime\) is the last step of continuation admission.

↩︎ Prefix tree management and KV-cache eviction policies are established in Prefix Caching Across Turns.

The final term marks a divergence between agent recovery and classical database crash recovery. In database systems like ARIES (Mohan et al. 1992), recovery time is dominated by reading the write-ahead log and redoing logged updates to dirty data pages on disk. In agent systems, deterministic log folding is cheap, because it executes local harness logic and updates in-memory tables without calling foundation models. Rebuilding the serving state, however, forces the system to recompute attention keys and values across the entire context history whenever no compatible cache prefix survives. Applying the weight-only prefill floor derived in Cost Asymmetry Between Input Prefill and Output Generation to the reference model and accelerator, the arithmetic compute floor alone for a 32,768-token context requires roughly 2.3 s of an accelerator’s peak 8-bit tensor throughput. This floor comes before accounting for memory bandwidth overhead, kernel launch delays, or queueing contention. Because attention state is strictly a function of the token prefix (principle 5), losing that cache throws away reusable computation while the causal knowledge survives safely in the log. Routing to a worker with compatible cached state can avoid prefix recomputation if the next prompt preserves that prefix; placement must also account for queueing, transfer delays, and available capacity. Systems engineers should profile each component of equation 7 before dedicating engineering effort to any single phase.

↩︎ Why prompt assembly must reproduce the delivered prefix token for token to keep cache reuse is established in Preserving an exact prefix.

Resume converts surviving durable records into an authorized continuation without misreading silence as failure. The same records support a second use that admits no new work at all, inspecting and testing what the trajectory already did.

11.6 Recorded Trajectory Replay

When an agent execution stalls or fails during an automated repair, resuming the session admits fresh work under current system conditions. Post-mortem debugging asks a different question, namely what the runtime recorded at the moment of failure and how the harness code responded to the inputs available at that time. Rerunning a targeted test like test_r18 under a new attempt cannot resolve that inquiry. Because external services drift, package versions change, and model sampling introduces variance, a new execution merely supplies fresh observations rather than reproducing the missing historical outcome.

Replay substitutes retained execution records for live, non-deterministic inputs. The guarantees provided by replay depend entirely on which portion of the system computation is repeated. Retained trajectories serve three distinct purposes (table 5): reconstructing a recorded state projection, which debuggers call time-travel replay; validating harness logic through regression replay; and branching into counterfactual experiments.

The first two modes make the same change to live execution. Where a live turn samples the model, passes the authorization gate, and dispatches the tool, replay disconnects the model and the tools and streams recorded replies and observations from the log into the harness (figure 4). They differ in what runs on those recorded inputs.

11.6.1 Projection reconstruction

When an engineer inspects a stalled trajectory to see what reservations were locked or what prompt bytes reached the model at turn 18, re-evaluating live policy rules or re-running prompt assembly would overwrite the historical state with today’s decisions. Projection reconstruction, the first replay mode, avoids that contamination by running only the reducer over the log as a deterministic event stream. Folding the log applies previously recorded decisions directly; today’s authorization policy never re-evaluates historical proposals. Starting from a known checkpoint record_R_ckpt, the compatible suffix reconstructs the selected record through its captured admission, reservation, intent, and outcome events.
