If the suffix ends abruptly after a ToolIntent record without an accompanying settling event, the resulting projection faithfully retains that in-doubt operation, exposing an interrupted execution.

Historical inspection can also retrieve the immutable input manifest captured for a specific ContextBuilt event. This manifest reveals the exact token sequence and bounded observations delivered to the model, rather than a prompt newly assembled from current, potentially mutated data sources. Reconstructing the corresponding workspace environment similarly requires mounting the compatible snapshot together with any incremental filesystem deltas logged up to that point.

Definition 11.3: Time-travel replay

Time-travel replay reconstructs a selected recorded state at a historical log boundary from compatible checkpoints, events, and retained artifacts, without repeating the original model calls or external actions.

  1. Significance: It renders the runtime’s historical evidence, decisions, and unresolved obligations fully inspectable at any retained log boundary for the cost of storage reads and a state fold, requiring zero model inference and generating zero external side effects.
  2. Distinction: Time-travel replay reconstructs an internal projection of recorded state, rather than restoring the full external world or re-evaluating decisions under updated policy rules.
  3. Common pitfall: Promising arbitrary historical inspection across every past turn when retention policies have already pruned required event payloads, filesystem snapshots, or historical reducer definitions.

11.6.2 Harness regression replay

Folding recorded decisions shows what the old harness decided, but it cannot test whether a code change in the harness state machine or prompt builder introduces a bug when handling the same historical inputs. Harness regression replay executes the harness code itself against captured external inputs rather than folding precomputed decisions through a reducer. Instead of querying live model endpoints or invoking real tools, the harness consumes recorded model completions, external tool observations, and control events. For deterministic reproduction, the captured boundary must supply all nondeterministic inputs consumed by the harness logic: wall-clock timestamps, randomly generated identifiers, configuration parameters, policy rules, and event arrival schedules. While this capture boundary does not need to simulate the entire operating system, any unrecorded source of entropy weakens reproducibility. This principle directly mirrors deterministic replay in operating-system debuggers, which record asynchronous hardware interrupts and system call outputs to allow developers to step backward through complex race conditions (King et al. 2005).

8 Happened-Before: Lamport showed that events in a distributed system are only partially ordered: one event precedes another when a chain of process steps and messages links them, and otherwise the two are concurrent (Lamport 1978). Parallel tool calls in one turn are concurrent in this sense, so a replay must either record the order the harness actually observed or declare that any order is equivalent.

Lamport, Leslie. 1978. “Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System.” Communications of the ACM 21 (7): 558–65. https://doi.org/10.1145/359545.359563.

To serve captured inputs correctly, the replay adapter follows the matching-request rule, matching every outgoing request against the recorded trace before returning a response. A captured model response is valid only for its specific prompt invocation and delivered token payload. Similarly, a tool observation corresponds to a unique logical operation and attempt, parameterized by exact tool names and serialized arguments. Replay adapters enforce matching using canonicalized representations or explicitly declared normalization schemes, combined with logged arrival sequences or formal concurrency models.8 A naive FIFO queue that blindly yields the next recorded event risks routing the test outcome of test_r18 to an entirely different operation or candidate patch. If an outgoing request deviates from the recorded trace, the replay engine must immediately fail the comparison; under no circumstances should it fall back silently to a live network call.

A reliable regression harness must seal all live side-effect pathways. Real tool dispatch, external callback handlers, asynchronous background reconciliation loops, and production database writes must be severed or redirected to in-memory mocks. While the test may mutate isolated, ephemeral test state, all interactions with external infrastructure are replaced by recorded observations. Enforcing these isolation boundaries is strictly the responsibility of the test runtime; the harness must never trust the model or agent code to refrain from issuing live operations.

Consider an edge case where an asynchronous tool returns an authenticated late result after an agent repair job has transitioned into the PAUSED state. According to the control-plane state machine, the correct harness behavior is to record the arriving observation in the operation table while keeping the agent paused, rather than automatically triggering a new model inference step. If a developer modifies the harness and inadvertently introduces a code path that fires an unauthorized model invocation upon receiving the late event, the regression replay immediately detects the discrepancy and fails the test. Successful execution against this historical trace proves only that the harness correctly handles that specific scenario; passing a replayed unit test for test_r18 does not constitute evidence of general task competence or end-to-end patch correctness.

When engineers update harness orchestration logic or tighten safety policies, behavioral changes are often intentional. Regression suites must therefore specify which outputs require strict byte-for-byte equivalence and which represent expected divergence. A diff between replayed execution and the historical baseline can indicate an unintended regression, an intentional algorithmic improvement, or a defect in the input capture itself. While replaying against recorded inputs eliminates expensive model API charges and external tool latencies, it still consumes local compute cycles, disk I/O, and setup overhead, and it validates behavior solely within the narrow envelope of previously observed execution paths.

11.6.3 Counterfactual branches

Regression replay must stop the moment a change alters the request the agent emits, so it cannot show what a trajectory would do next if an engineer modifies a prompt template, selects a different foundation model, updates an authorization policy, or changes an observation filter. To test such an intervention past the point where it changes the agent’s actions, the runtime forks execution by allocating a fresh branch identifier linked explicitly to the parent log sequence at a designated checkpoint boundary \(\CheckpointLogPosition\). It documents the intervention in metadata and reconstructs the historical execution environment to match that precise point in time. In the crash repair scenario, branching from checkpoint \(\CheckpointLogPosition\) requires mounting the exact workspace snapshot ws_r18 alongside matching dependency fixtures. If an engineer restores only the historical source code while executing against current, upgraded third-party packages, the experiment conflates the intentional prompt or code change with uncontrolled environment drift.

With durable logs, the runtime reconstructs the unchanged prefix deterministically through projection folding, completely avoiding redundant model sampling or tool execution up to the fork point. Beyond that boundary, however, the newly diverged continuation requires genuine observations that correspond to its altered actions. A captured historical response remains valid only as long as the outgoing request and environmental assumptions match the historical trace exactly. The moment the modified agent proposes a different test command, alters tool arguments, or produces an alternative patch candidate, the recorded downstream events become obsolete. At this divergence point, the branch must either halt replay or transition into active execution, gathering fresh observations from an isolated sandbox or compatible test fixture. Because the branch represents an unverified trajectory, production side effects remain strictly disabled, and human approvals granted to the parent execution never carry over automatically to the branch.

A counterfactual branch is thus a distinct, newly identified execution thread that inherits a validated historical prefix while exploring an alternative downstream path. Inspecting an individual branch helps engineers diagnose why a specific bug occurred or test how a targeted prompt patch performs on a known failure mode. Establishing broader claims about agent reliability or task success rates, however, requires running statistically grounded benchmarks across distribution-scale test suites. Replay establishes the precise boundary where historical evidence ends and new computation begins, and the same retained evidence can pinpoint the exact step at which two executions diverge.

↪ The statistical methods that turn repeated runs over a task distribution into reliability claims are developed in Statistical Evaluation Rigor.

11.7 Locating a Divergence

When an automated software-repair workflow undergoes regression replay, execution proceeds uneventfully until reaching the recorded targeted-test result for candidate patch r18. The stored test result itself remains identical, but a revised harness assembles a subtly different observation context for the subsequent model call. Waiting until the final repair verdict to compare the runs obscures this earlier discrepancy under layers of compounding tool calls and token generations. Effective debugging requires pinpointing the exact architectural boundary where two executions first cease to agree within the recorded evidence.

A divergence point is the first observable mismatch under a declared equivalence relation: between recorded and reconstructed state, between historical and revised harness logic on captured inputs, or between a golden trace and a live execution. Every comparison must explicitly define both its boundary and its equivalence criteria. In practice, two serialized JSON payloads might differ in a harmless trace identifier while representing the same permitted operation. Conversely, two tool invocations cannot be treated as equivalent simply because their shell commands match if they execute against different repository commits or working trees. A coarse turn counter obscures these distinctions when context assembly, model inference, authorization policy checks, and tool dispatch all unfold within a single turn.

11.7.1 Boundary-by-boundary comparison

Separating those four intra-turn stages turns a coarse turn diff into a precise fault isolator: if the tool output at the end of one turn matches the recording, the first mismatch in the next turn must appear at assembled model input, model response, harness admission decision, or tool observation. A boundary-by-boundary comparison isolates that earliest mismatch by starting at a verified checkpoint or initial state and stepping forward through corresponding events in their recorded sequence. Stable invocation and operation identifiers align the traces across runs, preserving any concurrency ordering or partial-order dependencies required by the replay contract. At each boundary, the diagnostic runtime evaluates three elements: the inputs delivered to that stage, the transformation configured for it (such as a prompt template, parser, or security filter), and the resulting output. Table 6 organizes this diagnostic evidence by the earliest architectural boundary where a mismatch surfaces, preventing engineers from confusing downstream symptoms with root causes.

In the r18 regression replay from the section opening, suppose the original trace recorded that r18 passed a targeted unit test, but noted that the broader regression suite was omitted. The original harness forwarded both statements to the model. During regression replay with an updated prompt builder, a modified context-compaction rule drops the scope limitation while preserving the word “passed.” Because the replay supplies the identical recorded test output and workspace snapshot, the first divergence occurs at the delivered context input, before the runtime invokes the model. This immediate mismatch isolates the defect to the context assembly change, which stripped the qualification evidence. Whether this omission subsequently misled the model into making a false completion claim remains a separate causal question for controlled experimentation; the divergence itself is already localized.

Localization granularity depends entirely on what artifacts survive in the persistent store. A surviving cryptographic digest confirms that bytes changed but hides the omitted text, while recording only the agent’s final answer conceals every intermediate boundary. An informative diagnostic report identifies both the last boundary that verified successfully and the specific missing record that halted further automated comparison, rather than attributing the failure to whichever component appears last in the execution trace.

11.7.2 Localization and explanation

Even after a boundary comparison flags a mismatch at a specific step, a hash difference alone cannot show which lines of prompt text changed, and matching hashes cannot explain why two identical prompts yielded different model replies. A cryptographic context digest verifies the byte-level integrity of the payload it covers, but it proves nothing about execution state outside that hash. Matching digests guarantee identical prompt text, yet sampling temperatures, decoding penalties, external tool schemas excluded from the hashed payload, model snapshot revisions, and provider configurations may still differ. Even when a visible request matches across every byte and parameter, hosted inference endpoints can still vary on the server. Conversely, unequal digests prove that the input representation changed, but explaining why requires inspecting the payload diff alongside its provenance metadata. Engineers must examine both the retained content and its lineage before drawing conclusions from hash comparisons.

Live model invocations can diverge even on identical prompts due to hardware and algorithmic factors. Lowering the temperature to zero or pinning a random seed standardizes the sampling policy, but it cannot eliminate variations from cluster-level accelerator scheduling, non-deterministic floating-point summation in parallel kernels, or silent provider-side model updates. Because an early divergence in a model response alters all subsequent prompts and tool invocations, downstream differences often reflect these cascading input changes rather than fresh errors. Debugging must center on explaining this initial point of departure.

↩︎ Floating-point reduction order and other sources of serving-path non-determinism are established in Repetition and replay.

Regression replay controls this cascading variance by supplying captured outputs at a declared boundary, following the principles developed in section 11.6. However, this isolation holds only if the harness captures every external dependency. If an authorization decision or scheduling check consults an unrecorded host clock, an unversioned environment variable, or a dynamic feature flag, determinism across that boundary collapses. When such leaks occur, developers must either capture the missing environmental inputs into the event log or narrow the replay scope to subcomponents with fully hermetic boundaries. Targeted interface interception in the harness achieves this control without requiring heavy, whole-machine snapshot infrastructure.

Sound diagnostic localization therefore yields a bounded engineering claim rather than an unverified causal leap. It specifies the earliest observed mismatch, catalogs the configurations and input artifacts confirmed to be identical, highlights the explicit differences found, and flags any missing evidence. While counterfactual replays can test whether a specific change explains the failure, observing an altered outcome in one trace does not prove that a fix behaves reliably across diverse workloads. Distribution-scale evaluation establishes the causal and statistical methodology needed to evaluate those system-wide claims; durable execution supplies the auditable evidence required to begin that inquiry.

Checkpoint 11.3: Replay and divergence

These boundary comparisons depend on which events, delivered inputs, and environmental artifacts remain accessible. An agent runtime’s retention policy therefore decides which recovery guarantees and diagnostic claims the engineering team can defend in production, which makes it more than a storage cost optimization.

11.8 Log Retention and State Compaction

An agent can reconstruct its execution trajectory from a checkpoint at sequence \(\CheckpointLogPosition\) and its subsequent suffix only as long as both the event records and their referenced artifacts remain intact. An event that points to a deleted workspace snapshot ws_r18 restores nothing. Conversely, retaining megabytes of raw compiler output is useless if the durable intent record for test_r18 was prematurely discarded. Retention policies must therefore stem directly from the platform’s recovery contract (the exact state and replay capabilities the system guarantees to support) rather than from storage format convenience.

11.8.1 Retention lifetimes

That recovery contract changes as a trajectory ages: an active run needs its latest checkpoint and in-flight operation table only until the task settles, whereas a regression test or compliance audit months later still needs the delivered prompts and tool outcomes, and raw profiler traces may be useful for only a few days of debugging. Expiring every artifact on a single timer either breaks long-window audit replay or forces the cluster to keep terabytes of raw telemetry for months. A production runtime must therefore separate three retention lifetimes: current recovery, historical replay, and optional diagnostics.

  1. Current recovery demands the minimum set of assets needed to resume a crashed agent immediately: the latest published checkpoint, the complete committed event suffix required to reconstruct the active state record and workspace, and every external artifact referenced by those records. It also requires the operation table that tracks in-flight operations, including attempt counts, mutual-exclusion reservations, and settlement owners. These operational obligations frequently outlive the worker process executing the task.
  2. Historical replay serves auditing, regression testing, and offline evaluation. Replay often requires earlier checkpoints, exact prompt templates, delivered model inputs, recorded completions, and environmental snapshots that online recovery has long since superseded.
  3. Diagnostics and telemetry capture ancillary metadata, such as raw network packet dumps, fine-grained profiler traces, or unparsed stdout streams that were filtered before reaching the model’s context.

Although these data classes overlap, each requires an independent retention schedule. A sound retention policy explicitly specifies the supported recovery boundary or replay window, the required artifact classes, the expiration triggers, and the subsystem responsible for settling unresolved work. A recovery guarantee holds only for data copies maintained within the declared failure domain. Pruning a required artifact prematurely breaks the recovery contract; retaining its cryptographic digest in a manifest does not preserve the missing bytes.

In the running crash scenario, record_R_ckpt, the workspace snapshot ws_r18, and the committed suffix through the ToolIntent for operation test_r18 (attempt attempt1) constitute mandatory recovery inputs. The missing tool result remains unknown until reconciliation completes. The runtime can safely prune an unrelated diagnostic log without compromising reconstruction under a contract that never uses it. In contrast, deleting ws_r18 destroys recovery entirely, because prompting the model to generate the patch again initiates a new, non-deterministic execution rather than restoring the candidate under test.

11.8.2 Content-addressed payload storage

Storing multi-megabyte payloads directly within sequential log envelopes quickly degrades storage engine performance and inflates write amplification. Content-addressed payload storage9 solves this by decoupling event metadata from bulk data: large payloads reside in dedicated blob storage, identified and verified by cryptographic content digests. Under this scheme, an event envelope remains compact, recording only the payload’s content digest, byte length, schema format, and storage location, alongside the bounded observation delivered to the agent model and its omission metadata. If that delivered observation is also offloaded to separate blob storage, it becomes a required recovery dependency as well.

9 Content Addressing: Venti used a hash of each block’s contents as its identifier, which made stored blocks write-once and let duplicate copies coalesce into one (Quinlan and Dorward 2002). Coalescing is what lets many trajectories over the same repository share a workspace base, and it is also why deletion must track references across tasks rather than per task.

Quinlan, Sean, and Sean Dorward. 2002. “Venti: A New Approach to Archival Storage.” Conference on File and Storage Technologies (FAST).

For bounded observations, the tool contract governs what the runtime captured, what it omitted, and what remains accessible through subsequent reads. While an unparsed raw tail might be optional for a particular replay target, a file delta, model input, or result body consumed by a state reducer cannot be treated as optional simply because it is large.

↩︎ The truncation notices and inspection handles that record what a bounded observation omitted, and what stays retrievable, are established in Truncation notices and inspection handles.

Write ordering between payloads and log envelopes matters for durability. A required payload must receive durable write acknowledgment from the storage system before any committed event envelope or checkpoint publishes a reference to it. Committing the reference first opens a crash window: if the worker fails while the payload write is still in flight, the log survives but records a dangling reference to missing bytes.

Upon retrieval, the runtime validates cryptographic integrity and checks tenant access permissions, because a content digest identifies stored bytes without granting permission to read them. Content addressing naturally deduplicates identical payloads across trajectories, allowing multiple runs on the same repository to share an immutable base workspace. However, task-specific provenance, confidentiality, and retention policies must remain strictly decoupled from storage deduplication. A shared payload can be reclaimed only when no active retention contract across any trajectory requires it, so expiring one task’s logs never deletes another task’s required dependency. Separating envelopes, per-turn payloads, and workspace snapshots in this way also makes the retention bill straightforward to price, revealing which storage classes actually drive operational costs.

Napkin Math 11.2: The price of keeping every recovery input
Problem: A repair trajectory runs 30 turns. Each turn appends 4 KB of event envelopes and 128 KB of delivered inputs and result bodies, and the trajectory pins one 50 MB immutable workspace snapshot. A representative standard object-storage tier costs $23 per TB-month. What does retaining every recovery and replay input for 12 months cost, relative to a hypothetical $2.10 attempt?

Math: The log holds 30 \(\times\) (4 + 128) KB = 3.96 MB, and the workspace brings the total to about 54 MB. Retention costs 0.054 GB \(\times\) $0.023 per GB-month \(\times\) 12 months \(\approx\) 1.5 cents.

Result: About 0.7 percent of the attempt’s cost, of which the log alone is 0.05 percent.

Systems insight: A year of full recovery and replay retention adds under 1 percent to \(\AttemptCost\) in equation 1.2, and the workspace snapshot, not the log, is 93 percent of the bytes. The bill grows linearly with retention months and bytes per turn, so the effective lever is sharing workspace bases by content address across trajectories of the same repository; truncating the log saves almost nothing. Set retention by the recovery and replay promise, because pruning a required artifact forfeits a reconstruction claim to save 0.05 percent of an attempt.

11.8.3 Log compaction

Keeping every event from the start of a trajectory grows the log without bound, even after a published checkpoint at sequence \(\CheckpointLogPosition\) has already captured record_R_ckpt and workspace ws_r18. Yet deleting that earlier prefix while the checkpoint is still staging, or while an audit replay still depends on it, breaks the runtime’s promises. Log compaction reclaims storage capacity safely by retiring the older prefix once a newer published checkpoint preserves the active state and all unresolved operations required by the current recovery contract. The retained suffix of subsequent events must connect seamlessly to the checkpoint’s exact committed sequence boundary. Before pruning any log records or referenced artifacts, the storage layer must verify both conditions: the replacement checkpoint and its dependencies are durable across the declared failure domain, and no active historical replay, auditing, or compliance obligation still depends on the older prefix.

Compaction preserves current recovery while deliberately shortening the historical replay horizon. Downstream systems cannot substitute a later agent context for an earlier one once the original inputs have expired. Similarly, squashing incremental workspace deltas into a single snapshot preserves the latest workspace tree while shedding intermediate revisions. A compaction policy must therefore explicitly define the surviving replay interval. An uncompacted event history grows monotonically with every turn, regardless of how tightly the agent manages its prompt token budget.

Systems engineers must not confuse log compaction with context compaction. Context compaction operates on prompt token budgets: when conversational history approaches model context limits, the runtime condenses prior turns and commits a Compacted event to the log. This event records the exact summary text delivered to subsequent model calls, preserving deterministic replay for future turns. The underlying historical events and payloads that generated the summary remain governed by their own retention policies rather than vanishing when the context window shrinks. Diagnostic telemetry can follow aggressive downsampling or retention rules; in contrast, recovery-critical events and payloads must remain bit-for-bit complete throughout their promised operational lifetime.

↪ Sampling and retention policies for diagnostic traces are developed in Trace Retention and Privacy.

11.8.4 The storage engine contract

A storage layer can quietly undermine durable execution if its ingestion mechanics treat every duplicate event key as a harmless network retry. When a worker retries a log append after a lost storage acknowledgment, the retried record carries the exact same envelope and payload bytes as the first attempt. If a bug or an unfenced zombie worker instead submits a timeout ToolResult for test_r18 under an event identity or sequence number that the store already holds with verified success evidence, the two writes share a key while disagreeing on what happened. A plain key-uniqueness constraint (such as an ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING clause) cannot tell those two cases apart, so it keeps the stored record and silently hides the conflicting write. Ingestion must instead perform semantic deduplication, comparing the immutable envelope and payload against the stored record under a declared canonical encoding. An identical retry returns an acknowledgment for the existing record. A conflicting payload under the same identity signals an integrity violation or a split-brain failure (two workers both acting as owner), not an idempotent duplicate, so the store must raise an error or record a distinct conflict event that the operation table evaluates. Deduplicating an event insertion, moreover, never deduplicates an external side effect that has already occurred.

The same discipline governs how the engine manages capacity and replication. When disk saturation or transient faults block new commits, the runtime must apply backpressure and stall the trajectory rather than proceed speculatively. A bounded circular buffer may overwrite an older entry only after every recovery and replay contract has released it. Moving older logs to a cheaper cold tier obeys the same completeness rule: the local copy can be retired only after every referenced payload body, not merely its index entry, has arrived and been verified at the destination. Replication across nodes must preserve both prefix completeness and ownership provenance. A follower that receives records out of order may stage them in memory, but it can advertise a recoverable prefix only up to its first sequence gap. Fresh appends must validate the active lease and fencing token to block zombie writes, whereas replicated historical events keep their original worker provenance and timestamps rather than taking on the credentials of the replicating node.

At the physical layer, an append-only event model can sit on any storage engine that guarantees deterministic ordering. Key-value stores built on log-structured merge trees (LSM trees) (O’Neil et al. 1996), for example, index immutable events under composite trajectory and sequence keys. In an LSM tree, storage-level compaction periodically merges the tree’s sorted on-disk files (sorted string tables) to reclaim space and speed reads. Despite the shared name, it is purely physical. It reorganizes bytes without changing the logical sequence of events, so unlike log compaction it never retires evidence. Overwriting an existing event with a newer state representation, by contrast, is a destructive application-level mutation, permissible only when a retention contract authorizes discarding the older evidence, because the order of observations and decisions is the agent’s causal chain.

Each cost derived earlier in the chapter therefore becomes a concrete requirement when selecting or configuring the storage engine. On the write path, the durable-acknowledgment boundary (local disk, quorum fsync, or multi-region replication) sets both the dispatch latency floor of table 2 and the batch wait of equation 2. The engine’s internal write amplification multiplies the snapshot bytes that equation 3 already counts. Its blob-store integration determines whether the payload-before-reference ordering of section 11.8.2 can be enforced in one place. On the recovery path, sequential read throughput governs the suffix read time \(\CheckpointSuffixReadTime\) in equation 7, while atomic compare-and-set support on the owner_epoch determines whether the log itself can reject a stale owner’s appends without an external lock hop.

A single relational or key-value store suffices for modest deployments. At higher throughput, architectures often split into an online recovery store for low-latency point lookups by trajectory and an offline analytical store for columnar scans over historical replays. The analytical copy may lag the primary only while a durable, synchronized copy already satisfies the recovery contract within the declared failure domain, and every additional copy needs its own prefix synchronization, completeness verification, and retention policy.

Checkpoint 11.4: Retention is a recovery promise

Retention completes the reconstruction contract by guaranteeing that committed evidence and required artifacts remain accessible after a failure. A storage engine faithfully preserves recorded uncertainties, but determining whether an external operation actually succeeded or produced correct side effects requires active reconciliation. Resolving in-flight ambiguities and reconciling orphan operations belong to effect-recovery policy, not to the storage engine.

11.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Building durable agentic systems demands clarity about what persistent state actually guarantees. The most consequential engineering mistakes confuse a surviving record with evidence it cannot provide, or expend heavy recovery effort where structured logging has already eliminated the need.

Fallacy: Saving the final context window is enough to audit or recover a trajectory.

A model’s final context window represents only the transient view passed to the last inference call, filtered through compaction policies and bounded observation budgets. It omits the operational decision history: which candidate proposals were admitted, which intents became durable, and which raw tool observations arrived and were subsequently summarized away. For example, if a tool returns an exposed credential at turn 7 and a compaction pass replaces that observation with an abstract summary at turn 12, the final context retains no trace of the leak. Comprehensive auditing and reliable crash recovery require the append-only event sequence defined in table 1. From an authoritative log with the required retained payloads, the execution engine can deterministically rebuild each captured past context window, whereas a surviving context window lacks the operational history needed to reconstruct the log.

Pitfall: Checkpointing after every turn to be safe.

Naively snapshotting full agent state after every turn introduces severe latency overhead for minimal operational gain. When turn-by-turn events and workspace mutations are already appended to a durable log, taking a full checkpoint only reduces replay time during recovery, yet it forces execution to pause on every step. In the crash trace of notebook 11.1, checkpointing after each of the 100 turns spends 52 s in cumulative capture and recovery time, compared to 6 s for a 25-turn stride. Engineers should tune this cadence with the logged balance of equation 5, grounding the calculation in the per-turn log reconstruction cost rather than the much slower runtime of live re-execution.

Fallacy: Rerunning the model at temperature zero reproduces a trajectory.

Greedy decoding fixes only the argmax token-selection rule for a given logit distribution, and the serving path that computes those logits is not numerically deterministic (Repetition and replay). Because autoregressive generation conditions every token on all preceding tokens, a single flipped token early in execution steers the entire subsequent trajectory into a different subspace.

Across multi-step agent horizons, trajectory reproduction compounds multiplicatively (equation 1.1). Even under the optimistic assumption that individual greedy calls reproduce their recorded outputs 99 percent of the time, a 30-turn trajectory matches its recording end to end only 74 percent of the time. That estimate ignores external non-determinism such as shifting system clocks, generated identifiers, and mutating external environments. Rerunning an agent prompt creates an entirely new execution trajectory that generates fresh side effects; reliable reproduction requires replaying the exact model completions and tool results recorded in the log.

Pitfall: Using an incomplete replica to conclude that a call was never sent.

In distributed systems, the absence of an event record in a replica does not prove that an action never took place. When recovery logic evaluates whether to re-dispatch an unconfirmed operation after a crash, inspecting an asynchronous read replica or diagnostic log can lead to duplicate side effects. If replication lag delays the arrival of an acknowledged intent, recovery logic reading that replica might incorrectly assume the request was never transmitted, even though the crashed worker had already dispatched the RPC to an external service. Re-dispatching on that false assumption duplicates external mutations. Recovery engines must verify prefix completeness directly against the authoritative, strongly consistent store before treating the absence of an intent as evidence of non-dispatch.

Fallacy: A durable intent, or a newer ownership epoch, makes an unresolved operation safe to settle or repeat.

Writing an intent record before dispatch guarantees that an operation is tracked, but it does not resolve the state of an in-flight network request. When an agent crashes with an operation marked in-flight, a missing response or recorded timeout leaves the physical outcome ambiguous. The crash might have occurred before the packet left the network interface, or after the remote service processed the mutation but before the acknowledgment arrived. Similarly, advancing an ownership epoch fences zombie workers from committing further updates to internal databases, but an epoch token cannot retroactively cancel a request already delivered to an external endpoint. Resolving an unconfirmed operation requires positive evidence obtained through the remote service’s inquiry API, and repeating it requires the endpoint’s idempotency contract. Settlement before retry (principle 8) applies after a crash exactly as it does after a timeout.

Pitfall: Resuming a recovered trajectory on whichever server is free.

Reconstructing an agent’s memory by folding an event log requires minimal CPU time and consumes no model tokens. Teams often make the mistake of budgeting recovery purely around log deserialization, scheduling the resumed agent on whichever inference node happens to report idle capacity. However, moving to a server without compatible cached state discards KV-cache locality. When the resumed agent issues its next model call, the target accelerator must recompute the attention state across the entire reconstructed context window. For a 32,768-token context on the reference model and accelerator, this cold prefill phase alone imposes a latency floor of roughly 2.3 s, even before accounting for network queueing delays. Prefix-aware dispatch compares the avoided prefill work against queueing, transfer delay, and capacity at eligible workers (equation 7), while preserving the exact prefix during prompt assembly.

11.10 Summary

Durable execution enforces a strict separation between what an agent runtime can verify locally and what actually transpires across external network boundaries. A runtime knows its own decisions, the dispatches it initiated, and the observations it received, provided each event reached storage provisioned to survive the declared failure domain before any dependent action occurred. What the runtime cannot deduce from internal logs alone is whether a dispatched request that yielded no response took effect in the outside world. Every mechanism examined in this chapter maintains this boundary. Durability must always be stated relative to a specific, named failure domain. Event log reconstruction replaces expensive model re-execution across recorded history. Ambiguous external outcomes remain explicitly assigned to an owner rather than resolved through speculative retries or uncoordinated model invocations. Providing these guarantees incurs concrete, quantifiable systems costs: a write acknowledgment per external dispatch, a brief coordination pause during checkpointing, a prompt prefill during cold resume, and about 1.5 cents per trajectory for a year of retention. Each overhead can be measured, budgeted, and amortized rather than avoided, and table 7 summarizes the governing relations that enforce dispatch ordering and balance checkpoint frequency against resume latency.

Key Takeaways: Preserve the evidence needed to continue
  • The event log, rather than the LLM context window, serves as the authoritative recovery record: A deterministic reducer folds recorded decisions, delivered inputs, and resource reservations back into the trajectory’s control state. In the event of a crash, recovery incurs the modest CPU cost of local log reconstruction rather than invoking redundant, nondeterministic model turns. The prompt context is merely a transient view assembled from this underlying history, and cannot reconstruct past state on its own.
  • Durability is meaningful only relative to a declared failure domain: An acknowledgment guarantees persistence only against the specific faults its underlying storage tier is provisioned to survive. Because the runtime must hold external dispatch until the intent is acknowledged, the write latency of that storage layer establishes the performance floor for every dispatch. An absent intent record proves that no dispatch took place only when the surviving log history can be verified as complete.
  • Fine-grained event logging shifts the checkpoint optimum: Classical checkpoint cadence balances capture overhead against lost execution time. Because replaying a deterministic event log is orders of magnitude cheaper than invoking new model calls, logging each turn lengthens the optimal checkpoint stride by the square root of the re-execution-to-reconstruction cost ratio. In the worked trace, the stride grows by a factor of 14.1×. Because the cost curve is broad and flat around this minimum, choosing a stride within a factor of 2 raises the cadence-dependent overhead by at most 25 percent.
  • Resuming an agent requires acquiring authorization rather than merely restoring memory: Reconstructed state explains why past decisions occurred, but the ownership lease, the fencing epoch, remaining token allowances, and human approvals govern subsequent actions. Any in-doubt external side effects and financial liabilities transfer intact to the new worker. A worker retaining compatible KV state avoids prefix recomputation, but the placement decision also depends on queueing, transfer delay, and capacity.
  • Deterministic replay serves a different diagnostic purpose than a live rerun: In regression replay, the test harness feeds recorded inputs back into the agent logic with live tool execution disabled, isolating the exact state boundary where behavior diverges. Conversely, re-running an agent against live models generates an entirely new trajectory; because model sampling and external environments drift, divergences during a rerun cannot isolate where the original execution went wrong.
  • Retention policies must reflect operational guarantees rather than raw storage costs: Fast crash recovery, regression replay, and post-incident auditing require different artifacts over distinct operational lifetimes. Long-term object storage is inexpensive. In the worked estimate, retaining every recovery and replay input for a full year costs about 0.7 percent of a single attempt. Eagerly pruning intermediate artifacts saves negligible budget while permanently forfeiting the ability to verify or reconstruct past runs.

The principle of intent before effect (principle 11) mandates writing an immutable record to durable storage before any external dispatch leaves the host, with durability calibrated against the failure domain the system claims to tolerate. Checkpoints and retained execution artifacts make this internal state reconstructible, while leaving in-doubt external outcomes explicitly visible to recovery supervisors. This discipline preserves the precise evidence that an automated or human recovery decision requires, while leaving the choice of recovery action to a separate policy that reads it. That separation also delineates the mechanical boundary of invariant closure (principle 1). The runtime can guarantee that no admitted effect escapes unrecorded and that no settled effect repeats without an explicit idempotency contract. However, local logs alone cannot determine whether an in-doubt external request succeeded across the network, nor can they judge whether a historically recorded action was semantically correct.

11.11 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Every mechanism in this chapter answers a question that transaction processing confronted first: how a system that crashes mid-update can determine afterward what work had already escaped. In his foundational 1978 notes on database operating systems, Jim Gray established the write-ahead log as the record from which recovery reconstructs committed work, while introducing the two-phase commit protocol to coordinate atomic updates across autonomous resource managers (Gray 1978). As databases moved into main memory, writing that log to stable storage emerged as the primary throughput bottleneck. DeWitt and colleagues addressed this limitation with group commit, amortizing disk synchronization barriers across concurrent transactions (DeWitt et al. 1984). Mohan and colleagues turned write-ahead logging into a comprehensive recovery discipline with ARIES (Mohan et al. 1992). ARIES established the rule of forcing each log record to stable storage before writing the modified page to disk. It also introduced the principle of repeating history on restart: the recovery manager reconstructs the exact state at the crash before undoing uncommitted changes. Gray and Reuter subsequently synthesized the formal transaction model, logging architectures, and crash recovery into a definitive treatise (Gray and Reuter 1992). Modern storage engines took this logic a step further by elevating the log from an auxiliary recovery file to the primary storage structure. Rosenblum and Ousterhout established this design with the Log-Structured File System (LFS), converting all file modifications into sequential append operations (Rosenblum and Ousterhout 1992). O’Neil, O’Neil, and Weikum later adapted that concept to database indexing with the log-structured merge-tree (LSM-tree) (O’Neil et al. 1996), establishing the append-only storage patterns that modern trajectory stores rely on today.

Gray, Jim. 1978. “Notes on Data Base Operating Systems.” In Operating Systems: An Advanced Course. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-08755-9_9.
O’Neil, Patrick, Edward Cheng, Dieter Gawlick, and Elizabeth O’Neil. 1996. “The Log-Structured Merge-Tree (LSM-Tree).” Acta Informatica 33 (4): 351–85.
Gray, Cary G., and David R. Cheriton. 1989. “Leases: An Efficient Fault-Tolerant Mechanism for Distributed File Cache Consistency.” Proceedings of the Twelfth ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP), 202–10. https://doi.org/10.1145/74850.74870.
Burrows, Mike. 2006. “The Chubby Lock Service for Loosely-Coupled Distributed Systems.” USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI).
King, Samuel T., George W. Dunlap, and Peter M. Chen. 2005. “Debugging Operating Systems with Time-Traveling Virtual Machines.” Proceedings of the 2005 USENIX Annual Technical Conference (USENIX ATC), 1–15.

A parallel lineage originated in high-performance computing and distributed systems, where jobs ran for days across fragile clusters and any node failure risked restarting computations from scratch. Young modeled the trade-off between the overhead of writing state snapshots and the cost of lost work, deriving an optimal checkpoint interval that remains the benchmark formula today (Young 1974). When computations spanned multiple communicating processes, capturing state became a distributed consistency problem. Chandy and Lamport solved this by defining snapshot consistency without freezing system execution (Chandy and Lamport 1985). Building on this foundation, the rollback-recovery literature demonstrated that combining periodic state snapshots with an event log of nondeterministic inputs guarantees deterministic replay, eliminating the need to re-execute expensive computations from the beginning (Elnozahy et al. 2002). Distributed environments also required explicit primitives to coordinate ownership and prevent split-brain execution during failover. Gray and Cheriton introduced time-bounded leases so that a crashed lock holder could not permanently block other workers (Gray and Cheriton 1989). Burrows introduced generation-stamped lock sequencers in Chubby, enabling external resources to reject requests from stale or zombie workers (Burrows 2006). Meanwhile, consensus protocols based on replicated state machines, from Lamport’s Paxos (Lamport 1998) to Ongaro and Ousterhout’s Raft (Ongaro and Ousterhout 2014), guaranteed that committed log records survive node crashes as long as a quorum persists. Capturing nondeterministic inputs also became a tool for systems debugging, as time-traveling virtual machines recorded external interrupts and network packets to replay operating system states deterministically backward and forward (King et al. 2005).

Agent runtimes synthesize these two lineages, yet they operate under distinct operational constraints that alter what recovery can promise. In an agent loop that interleaves model reasoning with tool invocations (Yao et al. 2023), the dominant nondeterministic event is the model’s generated output. Because stochastic token sampling and remote model endpoints cannot be assumed to reproduce identical outputs upon re-evaluation, the runtime cannot simply restart the agent loop from the beginning; it must log every generated prompt and completion as an immutable event. External tool execution introduces an even sharper departure from classic transaction processing: external endpoints do not participate in two-phase commit. When an agent invokes a web search, dispatches a payment API request, or modifies an external code repository, there is no prepare phase to hold resources in doubt, nor any global rollback coordinator to undo side effects. Large-scale distributed architectures encountered this same boundary decades earlier. Pat Helland articulated why cross-boundary distributed transactions fail at scale, showing that independent entities must instead communicate through idempotent messages, explicit sequence tokens, and compensating workflows (Helland 2007). Modern durable execution frameworks translated these distributed messaging guarantees directly into programming models: Burckhardt and colleagues provided a formal operational semantics for durable workflows that advance persistent progress without re-executing completed side effects (Burckhardt et al. 2021). In an agent architecture, these concepts become three runtime mechanisms: the durable intent that records planned actions before execution, the in-doubt state machine that reconciles unknown tool outcomes before retry, and the separation of historical trajectory reconstruction from live execution against the external world.

Yao, Shunyu, Jeffrey Zhao, Dian Yu, Nan Du, Izhak Shafran, Karthik Narasimhan, and Yuan Cao. 2023. “ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

For further reading, six works trace the lineage from database recovery to modern agent runtimes:

  • Gray and Reuter (1992) remains the definitive foundation for transaction processing, logging architectures, and crash recovery. Its rigorous analysis of what a commit guarantee actually promises, and across which specific failure domains that promise holds, provides the conceptual model for the failure-domain contracts developed throughout this chapter.
  • Mohan et al. (1992) delivers the complete specification for write-ahead logging and recovery. Studying ARIES clarifies precisely which consistency guarantees depend on owning the underlying storage pages, a structural guarantee database engines take for granted, but one that agent runtimes forfeit when interacting with external tool endpoints.
  • Elnozahy et al. (2002) organizes decades of distributed checkpointing and message logging research into a coherent taxonomy. The survey highlights why capturing nondeterministic inputs changes what a checkpoint must record, establishing the theoretical basis for deterministic trajectory replay.
  • Kleppmann (2016) connects classical principles of event sourcing, log replication, and distributed fencing to the realities of production infrastructure. It bridges distributed systems theory and the practical engineering of a scalable trajectory store.
  • Helland (2007) explains why large-scale distributed systems abandon atomic transactions across autonomous administrative boundaries in favor of idempotent, uniquely identified messages. Its practical reasoning directly anticipates the in-doubt action lifecycle and settlement-before-retry protocols required by agent runtimes.
  • Burckhardt et al. (2021) provides a formal operational semantics for durable execution and asynchronous workflow runtimes. It establishes how persistent progress can be mathematically specified and verified, showing how runtimes ensure exactly-once execution semantics across process crashes without manual state machine management.
Gray, Jim, and Andreas Reuter. 1992. Transaction Processing: Concepts and Techniques. Morgan Kaufmann.
Mohan, Chandrasekaran, Don Haderle, Bruce Lindsay, Hamid Pirahesh, and Peter Schwarz. 1992. “ARIES: A Transaction Recovery Method Supporting Fine-Granularity Locking and Partial Rollbacks Using Write-Ahead Logging.” ACM Transactions on Database Systems 17 (1): 94–162. https://doi.org/10.1145/128765.128770.
Kleppmann, Martin. 2016. Designing Data-Intensive Applications: The Big Ideas Behind Reliable, Scalable, and Maintainable Systems. O’Reilly Media.
Helland, Pat. 2007. “Life Beyond Distributed Transactions: An Apostate’s Opinion.” Third Biennial Conference on Innovative Data Systems Research (CIDR).
Burckhardt, Sebastian, Chris Gillum, David Justo, Konstantinos Kallas, Connor McMahon, and Christopher S. Meiklejohn. 2021. “Durable Functions: Semantics for Stateful Serverless.” Proceedings of the ACM on Programming Languages 5 (OOPSLA): 1–27. https://doi.org/10.1145/3485510.

11.12 Exercises

Each problem states its constraints and ends in a decision about what to record, when to checkpoint, or how to resume. Givens are hypothetical problem inputs unless a lighthouse profile supplies them, and none describes a particular store, model, or service.

1. Pricing the durable acknowledgment (Basic). A repair worker makes 40 tool dispatches over 40 turns, and each turn otherwise spends 4 s on model and tool work. Its store offers three acknowledgments: a returned write() after 0.02 ms, a returned fdatasync() after 0.4 ms, and a replicated append that a three-replica store acknowledges once two replicas hold the record durably. Measured from the append, the replicas become durable after 1.5, 2.5, and 6.5 ms, and the 1.5 ms replica keeps its copy on the worker’s own volume. For this problem, the worksheet requires both off-volume replicas to hold durable copies before dispatch.

Give the acknowledgment latency that satisfies each of the first two rows of table 2. Under this additional placement policy, identify the required copies and compute the resulting acknowledgment latency. What fraction of the trajectory’s elapsed time does that acknowledgment add? A colleague proposes ordinary majority commitment using the co-located replica and one off-volume replica. Compute the saving, distinguish the single-volume-loss guarantee from the chosen placement policy, and decide whether adopting that policy change satisfies the stated contract.

2. Write amplification of a growing record (Basic). A repair trajectory runs 400 turns, its checkpointed record grows by 64 KB per turn, and a checkpoint is published every 8 turns.

Use equation 3 to compute the bytes written by full snapshots and by deltas, and their ratio. Restore reads at 200 MB/s, and each delta in a chain adds 20 ms of fetch, validation, and application beyond its bytes. Starting from an empty base, compare the time to restore the state at the final checkpoint from one full snapshot and from the chain of deltas. Which representation suits a trajectory that rarely crashes, and which mechanism bounds its weakness?

3. Checkpointing too often (Basic). A long local analysis step inside a trajectory keeps intermediate state that the log does not capture, so a crash forces its work to be re-executed. Each checkpoint pauses execution for 3 s, including durable publication, and work-losing failures arrive on average every 30 minutes.

Compute \(\OptimalCheckpointInterval\) and the overhead at the optimum from equation 4. The current policy checkpoints every 10 s. Compute its overhead and the factor by which it exceeds the optimum. A second proposal checkpoints every 200 s to cut pause time. Compute its overhead, decide between the two proposals, and explain from the shape of the overhead curve why the comparison depends on the ratio to \(\OptimalCheckpointInterval\) rather than on the direction of the error.

4. Group commit against a dispatch budget (Intermediate). A harness admits 2,000 intents per second, arriving evenly every 0.5 ms. Its replicated store takes 5 ms per durable flush regardless of batch size. A batch starts its flush as soon as it holds its last intent and the store can accept a flush. The dispatch budget allows at most 8 ms from an intent’s arrival to its acknowledgment.

Without batching, with one flush at a time, what intent rate can the store sustain, and what happens to the queue? Find the smallest batch that keeps up, and use equation 2 to compute the steady-state waits of a batch’s first and last arrivals. Does that policy meet the budget, and how much utilization margin does it leave? The store can instead keep two flushes in flight at once. Recompute the smallest batch, its first arrival’s wait, and the amortized storage work per intent. A teammate suggests acknowledging from the page cache and flushing in the background. Evaluate the suggestion against the design rule that closes section 11.3.

5. What logging buys the cadence (Intermediate). A trajectory’s checkpoint write time is \(\CheckpointWriteTime = 0.8\) s, and crashes arrive at failure rate \(\TurnFailureRate = 0.01\) per completed turn. Reconstructing a logged turn costs \(\TurnReconstructionTime = 0.05\) s, and re-executing an unlogged turn costs \(\TurnReexecutionTime = 12\) s.

Compute \(\CheckpointStride^*\) for a fully logged and a fully unlogged trajectory, and their ratio. Show that the per-turn overhead at the optimum is \(\sqrt{2\,\CheckpointWriteTime\,\TurnFailureRate\,\TurnReconstructionTime}\) and evaluate it for the logged case. An audit then finds that 10 percent of turns write workspace files the log does not capture; in a lost suffix each such turn costs \(\TurnReexecutionTime\) and every other turn costs \(\TurnReconstructionTime\). Compute the effective per-turn cost, the new \(\CheckpointStride^*\), and the overhead of keeping the logged stride. Which investment lowers overhead more, retuning the stride or capturing the missing workspace deltas? Justify the choice with the three overheads.

6. Second lighthouse: mass resume for the support agent (Intermediate). The support agent holds many short sessions at once. Each session’s context is 8,192 tokens, of which 6,144 form a policy prefix shared by every session, and a customer takes about 20 s to read a reply and answer. A serving region fails, and 2,000 open sessions must resume on surviving workers. Per session, acquiring ownership takes 20 ms, reading and validating the checkpoint and suffix 15 ms, and folding 5 ms. There is no workspace to restore, because the customer record stays in its system of record, but 10 percent of sessions hold an in-doubt refund whose authorized lookup takes 150 ms. One request prefills at 20,000 tokens/s, and the surviving pool prefills 400,000 tokens/s in aggregate. The failover may add at most 1 s to any customer’s next reply.

Use serial stage execution for the per-session estimate. Apply equation 7 to compute \(\ResumeTime\), excluding queueing, for a clean session and for one with an in-doubt refund, first with a cold prefill and then with the shared prefix already resident on every surviving worker. If every session resumes eagerly with a cold prefill, how long does the pool take to drain the prefill work, and with the shared prefix resident? Treat customers’ next messages as arriving evenly over the following 20 s. Propose a resume policy that meets the 1 s bound, compute its prefill load as a fraction of the pool, and state what it does with the in-doubt refunds. Contrast the binding term with the one that governs the repair trajectory’s resume.

7. Second lighthouse: retention for the research agent (Intermediate). The research agent runs 20 turns and fetches 100 external pages. Per turn it logs 4 KB of event envelopes and 40 KB of delivered, bounded observations. Each fetched page averages 300 KB raw, and the model receives only the bounded observation. Storage costs $0.023 per GB-month, the replay promise is 12 months, and one attempt costs $1.50.

Compute a year’s retention cost for the recovery and replay inputs and for the raw pages, each as a share of the attempt cost. An investigator later asks which text the model received before producing a suspicious citation. Decide whether folding the recorded projection, a harness regression replay, or a fresh rerun of the trajectory establishes the delivered input. Name the earliest boundary to compare and the retained bytes it needs, and explain why neither the raw pages nor the rerun can settle it. State what that evidence cannot establish about the text’s causal influence, and what a controlled new branch would add. Propose retention lifetimes that keep raw pages for 1 month under a diagnostic policy, and compute the annual storage bill for one million trajectories under full retention and under the proposed policy. Explain how the dominant bytes, and the reason for retaining them, differ from the repair trajectory’s in notebook 11.2.

8. An asynchronous replica behind an irreversible actor (Advanced). A fleet of 50 remediation workers each dispatch approved changes at 2 per hour, at random times, after an approval wait of about 15 minutes. Each worker acknowledges intents with a local fdatasync() and ships them to a remote store that lags by 200 ms. After a failure, the replacement reads the remote store and dispatches every approved proposal that lacks an intent. Each worker suffers 2 volume-losing failures per year, and a duplicated change costs $12,000 in expected damage. The alternative acknowledges from a quorum outside the worker’s volume at 6.5 ms per dispatch.

Identify the rules of section 11.3 that the recovery procedure violates, and state the recovery state that operations in the lag window should occupy instead. Treating dispatches as a Poisson process, compute the probability that a failure follows within 200 ms of at least one dispatch, the expected number of such failures per year across the fleet, and the expected annual loss if each produces a duplicate change. Repeat for a network partition that stretches the lag to 60 s. Compute the latency the quorum alternative adds per worker per day, and per change as a fraction of the approval wait. Redesign the acknowledgment and recovery path, and defend why neither acknowledgment latency nor the small expected loss at 200 ms is the right quantity to optimize for this lighthouse.

9. Consolidating a delta chain under a resume deadline (Advanced). A repair trajectory publishes an incremental checkpoint at every stride and, every \(\ConsolidationStride\) checkpoints, consolidates the chain by writing and publishing a new base, which pauses execution for 1.0 s. The chain therefore holds between 1 and \(\ConsolidationStride\) deltas. A restore reads the base in 0.2 s and spends 0.04 s on each delta in the chain. The probability that a restore is needed between two consecutive checkpoints is 0.005.

Write the expected overhead per checkpoint \(\ConsolidationOverheadTime(\ConsolidationStride)\) as a consolidation term plus an expected restore term, taking the chain length at a crash to average \(\ConsolidationStride/2\). Find \(\ConsolidationStride^*\) and \(\ConsolidationOverheadTime(\ConsolidationStride^*)\) by the method of theorem 11.1, and compute the worst-case restore time at \(\ConsolidationStride^*\). Resume now must finish restoring within 2 s in the worst case. Find the largest admissible \(\ConsolidationStride\) and the overhead the deadline costs relative to \(\ConsolidationOverheadTime(\ConsolidationStride^*)\), and check the ratio of the \(\ConsolidationStride\)-dependent terms against the flat-minimum form \((\OptimumMissFactor + 1/\OptimumMissFactor)/2\). When a consolidation publishes a new base, which older deltas and bases may compaction delete, and what must be verified first?

What’s Next: From reconstructing the record to repairing effects
If the log faithfully records every intent and observation, why can a recovered trajectory still leave the world half changed? Reconstruction restores what the runtime decided and saw, but it cannot settle a test_r18 job whose outcome is still unknown, decide whether revising candidate r18 is worth another attempt, or tear down an environment that a lost reply left running and billing. Failure Recovery turns the recorded evidence and unresolved obligations into recovery policy: retries that an endpoint’s repeat contract permits, bounded forward repair, compensations registered before the effects they undo, and escalation to an operator, together with watchdogs that stop trajectories which fail without raising any error.
